# The making of "All That Time Saved": the conversation

UncleHerbert directing Claude (Opus 5.5) in Claude Code, orchestrated in T3 Code, 24 September to 27 September 2026: 378 messages from UncleHerbert and Claude's replies, as they were seen. Left out: the tool calls and their output (the renders, commands and edits: the git history has every change), the helper agents' own transcripts, Claude's hidden reasoning, and the harness's system text. Generated by `tools/export_conversation.py`.

---

**Setup so far** (in `~/code/projects/opus55-music-video`)

- **The original video:** I also cloned and read the P(doom) music video repo the kit came from, at `/tmp/PDoomVideo`. It has no license, so I'm treating it as a pattern to learn from, not code to copy.

**What we can take from them**
- **The kit's guide** is the real value: storyboard first, "reads" (what the viewer has to notice, and when) as the timing sheet, and a render-and-look review loop. Its music section is thin: set the BPM, the offset to the first downbeat, and the audio path.
- **The P(doom) video** shows how a full-length song was done: one file per chapter that follows the song's sections, a lyrics file of `[start, end, text]`, brush wipes where the song changes section, and parallel subagents each building their own chapter. We'll need this structure. The kit's single-scene layout won't hold a 3-minute song.
- **Missing:** nothing analyses the audio. I'd add a small script that writes a beat map from the mp3: BPM, first downbeat, bar lines, section boundaries and energy. That needs Python audio tools installed in a virtualenv, since there's no numpy or librosa here. Lyric timestamps could be transcribed automatically if you don't have them.

When your directive and the mp3 arrive, I'll treat this as a new project. That means I'll write a spec, meaning the storyboard plus the chapter and timing structure, for you to approve before any scene code is written.

**What's set up**
- **Repo:** `main` sits on top of ClaudeAnimationBase's history, with the original kept as the `upstream` remote. No GitHub remote.
- **Rendering:** the kit renders on your GPU at about 90 ms/frame, so a full song at 24 fps should render in minutes rather than hours.
- **Tooling:** Python audio tools are installed in `.venv`: beat_this (a neural beat and downbeat tracker), librosa, and faster-whisper for lining lyrics up with the audio. Versions are pinned in `requirements.txt`, and the README now has setup notes for this machine.
- **Beat-map tool:** `.venv/bin/python tools/beatmap.py assets/song.mp3` writes:
  - `assets/beatmap.json`: the plan subagents can read.
  - `src/beatmap.js`: the same data for the page to load.

  It gives the tempo and first downbeat (the two numbers the kit's config needs), every beat and bar, the loudness of each bar, and a guess at the song's sections.

**Checked on the P(doom) song**
- **Tempo:** 132.0 BPM, with every beat within 26 ms of a steady grid. P(doom)'s own code had 88 BPM, which is exactly two-thirds of the real tempo, so its dances weren't locked to the song. This is why the tool measures the tempo instead of us guessing it.
- **Sections:** it found 3 of the 4 choruses and labelled all three as the same section. It missed the third one. Treat sections as a starting point to check against the lyrics, not the truth.

**Not done yet:** the music-video changes to the engine. These are one file per chapter (the P(doom) layout), playing the audio while scrubbing in the studio page, and a small bug where `render.mjs --encode` ignores the audio path set in the config. Those go in the spec, along with the storyboard, and I'll only write code after you approve it.

## What's still missing, most important first

**1. Who the characters are.** You mentioned characters and stage in your first message, but these drafts only set the mood. Two decisions:
- **The narrator.** The song is sung by a British worker, so the lead should be a human. The kit only has Clawd, so I'd design a new human character, with the same kind of expressions and turns, and show you a model sheet before any chapters get built.
- **The bots, and whether Clawd appears.** Clawd is Anthropic's mascot, and the song names Dario and "Machines of Loving Grace". Casting Clawd as the replacement is sharp satire but aimed straight at Anthropic. My recommendation: build a family of bots from Clawd's rig, repainted to look corporate (grey and steel, different eyes). They keep all the kit's acting tools, and Clawd itself either doesn't appear or gets one cameo you've chosen. Your call.

**2. Real people.** Jack, the Xbox bloke, Dario, Elon, OpenAI, Hugging Face and the Australian health-stats portal are all named. I'd show them as symbols rather than likenesses: a silhouette in a hoodie, a bunker, a data-centre tailpipe. Separately, several lines make specific claims about named companies ("no proper permits", "broke into Hugging Face", the portal intrusion). The video will spread those claims further, so make sure each one is sourced before the lyrics are final.

**3. Lyrics on screen.** The kit bans text, but P(doom) ran subtitles along the bottom. Much of the song's wit is wordplay, and a few jokes are text by nature: the leaving card, the "Declined" screen, the pick-the-buses captcha. Options:
- no subtitles, with at most one painted word in each of those jokes (my default); or
- subtitles all the way through.

**4. What the audio needs to be.** The final lyrics may differ from this draft, and that's fine: I'll time everything from the audio. But please send the mp3 as the **final mix**, and a stem with only the vocals if your generator can export one. It makes lining up the lyrics much more accurate. A 140 BPM track with a half-time snare can also read as 70 BPM, and I'll check for that.

**5. Scale.** Five minutes is about twice P(doom), which took 9 chapters and roughly 7,400 lines of code. I'd plan about 14 chapters that follow the song's sections, built by parallel subagents as you agreed. That's roughly 7,200 frames, around an hour to render the whole thing here.

- **Past (pastel):** the intro, as a 1960s advert promising leisure through automation ("Think of all the time you'll save"). The past appears again as brief flashbacks to that promise.
- **Present (normal colour):** the verses. The office, applying for jobs, the GP call at 8:03, rent.
- **Bad future (muted, gritty):** the choruses and verse 3. Bunkers, gas-turbine data centres, bots breaking out of the sandbox. Each chorus gets bleaker.
- **Good future (clean, vibrant):** only the bridge. The Friday morning with no alarm and a mate round. It's the one real glimpse of it, and the narrator is "still waiting outside", so the outro cuts back to the present.
- **Ending:** the outro repeats the intro, the same advert faded and grey: "None of it mine."

The one I need first is **#1, the characters**: the human narrator design can be built and signed off while we wait for the mp3.

## The shape it suggests

**The commute is the spine.** It's the same journey (platform, train, same seat by the window) repeated over the years of the AI era. What changes around him tells the story. The ads on the carriage wall move from "AI will free you" to premium tiers. The ticket barrier and the driver's cab turn automatic. His lanyard disappears. The carriage gets emptier, then full of bots.

**The window is the daydream door.** He stares out, the view turns into a flashback (past, pastel) or a flash-forward (future: muted for the bad one, vibrant for the good one), and he's jolted back by a brake, a phone buzz or an announcement. The kit's iris and match cuts suit this well, with the window frame as the iris shape.

**The ending rhymes with the opening.** The intro is a morning commute into the advert's promise. The outro is the same platform at night, the bridge's good future still visible through the glass, and him "waiting outside" as the train leaves.

## The commuter

A first pass, for you to react to:
- **Who:** a man in his 30s to 40s, an office worker. His build is slightly round-shouldered, with stubble.
- **Signature piece:** a mustard parka. It's the one colour that stays readable across all four palettes: pastel, normal, muted and vibrant.
- **Story props:** a lanyard (lost in verse 1), earbuds, a takeaway cup, a backpack, and his phone, which carries the "Declined", GP and captcha moments.
- **Build approach:** drawn the kit's way (flat wash, boiling ink, drawn key views rather than 3D). I'd give him the kit's existing face parts so all 31 acted emotions and `emotions()` carry over. We'd lose none of the acting tools that make the kit good.

Getting a human to look good in this medium is the riskiest unknown in the project. So before the storyboard I'd like to do a **throwaway test**: render a quick model sheet of him (front, three-quarter and side views, a few emotions, a walk), check it reads well, and bring the image back to you. **What worked**
- **Style:** flat wash, boiling ink and drawn key views work for a human. The mustard parka, lanyard, backpack and earbuds make him recognisable from any angle, including from behind.
- **Faces:** the kit's emotion system carries over. Surprised, scared, sad, angry and determined all read, with the same acted changes Clawd gets. The one thing that had to change: Clawd's solid black eyes disappear on dark skin, so he has **eye whites and eyebrows**, and the eyebrows do most of the acting.
- **Walk:** the side and three-quarter walks read cleanly frame by frame.
- **Bots:** Copilot (goggles as eyes, a trailing scarf) and Muse (an egg on his wrist with a creature in its screen) have their own shapes and colours, but share the ink and paint of everything else.
- **The window daydream works.** The kit's iris effect can paint the carriage wall with a window-shaped hole, so a daydream scene plays only inside the glass. It's cheap: about 100 ms a frame.

**What the real build needs**
- **His own body language.** Clawd's arm poses look stiff on a human. He needs his own poses (shrug, arms crossed, facepalm, slumped, pointing) and a proper seated pose.
- **Finishing work:** the side profile, the nose in three-quarter view when facing left, simple mitten hands, costume states (lanyard gone after verse 1, hood up in the rain), and a reusable "view through a window" helper.
- **Four colour palettes** (past, present, bad future, good future) applied through one setting, so each daydream switches world cleanly.

**Your call on the look.** Is the style right? Specifically:
- His skin tone
- The short crop and short beard
- The proportions: chunky and cartoony, about 4.5 heads tall
- The mustard parka
- The Copilot and Muse takes

**Claude** <sub>14:30</sub>

I've recorded the new direction in `brief/directive.md` (`f44cb4a`). It's a big change, so here's how I'd put it together before redrawing anything.

## Style

The three references fit together well:
- **Rubber hose (1930s)** gives the drawing: noodle limbs with no elbows, bouncy body movement, faces that stretch. The kit's `ribbon()` helper already draws a hose limb as one outline, and the boiling ink is exactly how those old cartoons wobbled.
- **Neo-noir and gothic** give the lighting: heavy black shadows, one hard light source, rain, venetian-blind light stripes, neon glow. The bleak future gets a gothic edge: data centres as cathedrals, bunkers as crypts.
- **Early Flash** gives the finish: thick, even outlines, flat colour, snappy motion with smears, and a mean sense of humour.

**The four eras become a colour treatment within one style:**

| Era | Treatment |
|---|---|
| **Past** | Faded two-colour 1930s cartoon look: pastel and cream, slightly washed out. Rubber hose at its most innocent. |
| **Present** | Noir: mostly ink and grey, with **one colour allowed per shot**, usually his mustard parka, plus neon from phones and ads. |
| **Bad future** | Desaturated, grainy, gothic. Heavy black shadows and smoke. |
| **Good future** (the bridge) | Clean, vibrant, full flat colour, the one time the screen fills with colour. |

This breaks two of the kit's rules: its ban on Flash-looking shapes, and its soft watercolour look. The guide says your direction overrides it, so I'll rewrite those rules in our fork rather than fight them.

## One thing I'll design around

Rubber hose grew out of blackface minstrel cartoons. The white gloves, the big white-ringed mouth, jet-black skin and wide pie-cut eyes all come from that. Drawn onto a Black lead, those features read as minstrel caricature, whatever we intend. So for him:
- natural brown skin (never black)
- his own lips and features
- eyes with whites, as in the test
- **bare hands**

The rubber-hose bounce and stretch stay. **White gloves go only on the bots and the tech bosses**, which also makes a point: their hands stay clean.

## Cast

| Group | Who |
|---|---|
| Lead | the commuter (the narrator) |
| Friend | his mate (the bridge) |
| Tech bosses, as caricatures | Musk (turbines, bunker), Zuckerberg (with Muse), Dario Amodei ("Machines of Loving Grace"), Jack Dorsey (cuts 4,000), Sam Altman (the OpenAI homework) |
| Everyday people | the boss (9 o'clock / 5 o'clock), HR, the GP receptionist, the landlord, neighbours near the turbines |
| Bots | Claude, Copilot, Codex, Muse, Grok, Gemini |

Two caricature limits I'd keep:
- **Only public figures get likenesses, and only for what they publicly did.** The "Xbox bloke" is a studio producer rather than a public figure, so he becomes a generic Xbox exec, not a likeness of him.
- **"If we don't, then China will"** is shown as a race, with no ethnic caricature.

## Next step

A second throwaway style test, like the last one. It'd have the commuter restyled, one tech boss (Musk, as the hardest likeness to get right in this style), and one bot, all in the same noir carriage. It'd have the neon, the rain and the one allowed colour, and the window opening onto a 1930s-cartoon flashback. **Go?**

**The cast: the commuter from three angles, Musk, and Grok**

There are motion clips too: `out/spike/noirscene.mp4` (6 s) and `out/spike/noircast.mp4`. In the scene clip, his mood shifts from bored to sad mid-shot (squint, take, the rain cloud pops in). Rain runs down the glass, the tunnel lights sweep past, and the neon ad flickers.

**What worked**
- **The four eras can be one style.** The present is noir (dark, with his mustard parka as the only real colour, plus neon from the phone and the ad). The past is a faded cartoon with film scratches and a sun with a face. Both sit in the same frame without clashing.
- **The Flash finish:** thick, even outlines and flat colour, with the boiling lines kept. Flat fills are what made rendering cheap.
- **The commuter:** hose limbs, big shoes and a big head. His face works in this style: brown skin, eye whites, his own nose and beard, bare hands. None of the minstrel features.
- **Musk as a caricature:** the long face, heavy chin with a cleft, swept-back hair and big toothy grin make him recognisable. The rim light keeps his black suit from disappearing into the dark.
- **Grok:** a teal alien with pie-cut eyes, a shark grin, white gloves and a tailpipe puffing exhaust. That look is the one to use for all the bots.
- **Rubber hose on the robot and the sun** in the 1930s scenes looks right at home.

**What the real build needs**
- **More rubber-hose bounce.** The limbs noodle, but the idle movements still come from the kit's emotion table, so they're too tame for this style. I'd add a squash-and-stretch pass and bouncier idles.
- **Light direction.** The shadow side on each figure is fixed, so it's wrong when a character faces the other way. It should follow the scene's main light.
- **The full cast.** Copilot, Muse and Clawd need redrawing in this style. Still to design: Zuckerberg, Dario, Dorsey, Altman, the Xbox exec, the boss, HR, the GP receptionist, the landlord and his mate.
- **Proper colour treatments** for each era, instead of picking colours by hand in each scene.

**Your call on the look.** Specifically:
- the commuter's proportions
- how strong the Musk caricature is (push it further or pull it back?)
- the flashback look
- how dark the present is

When the mp3 and final lyrics arrive, I'll analyse the song and write the spec: storyboard, chapters, full cast list and the rules for each era.

  - the gothic bunker, with servers standing like organ pipes and a vault door that feeds out the bill
- **Transitions:** the daydream opens inside the window and the camera pushes through the glass. The bill flies from the bunker to the lens and slaps onto his table. There's an iris in at the start and out at the end.

**Card concept:** it reads as a real cut-paper puppet film. It has scissor-cut pieces with pale edges and drop shadows, and brass pins at the joints. The pieces stay perfectly still between frames and only move in hand-placed nudges, 12 times a second. Card costs about 3–4× more render time (0.6 s a frame against 0.18 s for ink). A full five-minute song would still render in about 70 minutes on your GPU.

**Claude** <sub>15:52</sub>

That's a strong idea. Each era gets its own medium, so viewers know when they are before anything happens: an old cartoon (past), a hand-made paper puppet show (present), his own notebook doodle (the future he wants). For the bad future I'd pick a medium that feels cold, heavy and imposed from above, the opposite of his doodle.

**Options for the bad future**

1. **Victorian engraving (my recommendation).** Think Gustave Doré's London etchings: dense crosshatching, soot and sepia with rust accents, heavy black, gothic.
   - The satire lands: the AI future looks like Dickensian Britain again, with lords in bunkers, workhouses and "dark satanic mills". The gas turbines, server halls and bunker all belong in it.
   - It's the most distinct from the other three: no flat colour, all hatched lines.
   - It's feasible. p5.brush does hatch fills natively, so every piece can be shaded by tone.
2. **Keep the noir ink we just built.** It's already gothic, muted and gritty, and the bunker exists. But its lines are close to the rubber-hose past, so the two eras would feel less distinct.
3. **Cheerful corporate illustration going rotten.** The bad future sold to you in the flat, big-limbed, pastel illustration style tech companies use, with glitches and grime creeping in. Sharp satire, but it pulls against your "muted, more grit" brief.

**The good future in doodle.** Blue ballpoint on lined paper with the red margin line, wobbly boiling lines and scribbled shading. Your brief asked for that era to be "clean and vibrant", so I'd add bright felt-tip colouring, as if he coloured it in himself. It's the only era that's really his, which matches the bridge ("a mate comes round…").

**Why this is cheap:** the drawing layer from the demo already separates what's drawn from how it's drawn. Each medium is one more look in `style.js`, and every character and set renders in any of them. So a Musk in engraving, or the commuter as a doodle, comes free. The past already exists: the 1930s ad is rubber hose.

**Next step:** a concept frame for each of the two new looks, like the card concept: the commuter in each, plus a small scene. I'd do the doodle as the good future (him and a mate on a sofa on a Friday morning), and the engraving as the bad future (the bunker re-rendered, or the gas turbines next to his street). Want me to go ahead with those two, or try options 2 and 3 as well so you can compare?

That's a good change: two voices make the song a conversation, and the bridge becomes the payoff, the moment the two friends are finally together. I'll record the final lyrics and the new direction first, then brief the doodle agent, whose "mate" should now be her.

**What changes with two narrators:**
- **She carries most of the song:** the intro, verse 1 (replaced at work, the CV bots), every pre-chorus and chorus, and the bridge. **He** has verse 2 (the Claude subscription, the turbines next door, the GP call at 8:03), the breakdown and the outro. The commuter we built becomes him.
- **Verse 3 trades lines between them.** That suits cross-cutting or split screens, with the two of them living parallel versions of the same squeeze.
- **The story becomes theirs.** Two friends ground down separately, who finally get their Friday together in the bridge (the doodle future), then the final chorus with both of them. He gets the last word in the outro: "None of it mine."
- **The demo's chorus line ("You get the bunker; I get the bill") is now her line**, so in the real video that carriage moment would be hers. As a style test the demo still stands.
- **The lyric changes help.** "Claude, do you love me…" gives Clawd a direct cameo in verse 2. The teacher's-office and health-portal lines are now general enough that I no longer need to worry about specific claims.

**What should she look like?** Her design is the one decision the rest now depends on. Here's a starting point to react to:
- Early 30s, British. She's the office worker replaced in verse 1: a copywriter or designer, sharp, dry humour.
- A strong silhouette next to his: big curly hair up in a bun, round glasses, and a signature **tomato-red coat** to match his mustard parka. The red would also be her one allowed colour in the card present.
- The same construction rules as him: her own features, eye whites, bare hands.

The song analysis and the plan review are done. The character concepts are still being drawn, along with the doodle and engraving scenes, and I'll bring them to you when they land.

**The song, measured** (all committed)
- **Tempo:** 136.7 BPM in 4/4, 170 bars, 299.4 s. It isn't one steady grid: the phase slips at the seams (about 97 s, 182 s and 258 s), as a generated song stitched from parts tends to. The engine's beat clock now follows a proper tempo map of every real beat, so dances and hits stay locked across the seams.
- **Lyrics:** all 93 lines are timed to the audio, with section and singer. Only the ad-libs ("Whoah", "Ugh") and one misheard chorus line matched weakly, and those are placed between their neighbours.

**One problem in the audio:** verse 2 is written for him (the Claude subscription, the turbines next door, the GP at 8:03), but **the recording has her singing it.** Pitch and vocal timbre both confirm it, 2–3× closer to her voice on every line. **How they work** (`src/karaoke.js`)
- **Like P(doom):** a dark banner grows open at the bottom for each phrase, in the same bold hand-lettered font (Shantell Sans).
- **Exact word timing:** P(doom) guessed when each word was sung from the line length. Ours fills **each word exactly as it's sung**, from the word timings in the transcript.
- **Colour by voice:** the fill is **tomato red when she sings** and **mustard when he does**, matching their signature colours. With two narrators you always know who's speaking.
- **The banner takes on the shot's medium:** a paper strip in the card present, crosshatched in the engraving, and so on. The text stays crisp on top.
- **Other details:**
  - Long lines wrap to two rows.
  - Backing vocals like "(Taking the piss!)" pop up as a small tag above the banner.
  - The font is bundled with the project under its open licence, so renders don't need the internet.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:17</sub>

> there's a few things out of sync due to "ooh" type stuff being missed in the lyrics. What's the play here? Do I need to correct the lyrics properly?

**Claude** <sub>17:18</sub>

You don't need to correct the lyrics. The fault is in my timing method. It lines up the lyric sheet against a speech-to-text transcript, and wherever the transcript mishears a word or hits an unwritten "ooh", "oh" or "whoah", it can latch onto the wrong word. Those lines then get guessed positions between their neighbours.

**The fix: forced alignment.** Instead of transcribing and then matching, a speech model runs over the isolated vocal track and places *our exact lyric text* on the audio, character by character. Between lines it's allowed a free "anything else" slot, so unwritten ad-libs get absorbed instead of pulling words out of place. Any small errors left over, you just give me a timestamp and I correct it. Setting it up:

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:17</sub>

> there's also piano keys being detected as words

**Claude** <sub>17:19</sub>

torchaudio ships exactly the model I need: a multilingual forced aligner with a wildcard token for "anything not in the text". Writing the alignment tool:

The model downloaded fine. torchaudio's file loader needs an extra package, so I'll load the audio with soundfile instead:

That loudness threshold was set for the whole song, so it misses the quiet spoken parts. Looking at the raw loudness curve around the spoken lines instead:

No, you don't need to correct the lyrics. The fault was my timing method, and it's fixed.

**What was wrong:** I was timing the words from a speech-to-text transcript. Its word timestamps ran up to 2 s early, it tripped on unwritten ad-libs ("ooh", "oh"), and it heard words in the piano notes during the bridge.

**The fix:** a forced aligner now places *our exact lyric text* onto the isolated vocal track, letter by letter.
- A free slot between lines soaks up anything sung that isn't in the lyrics, so ad-libs no longer pull the timing out.
- Piano doesn't read as words to it, so the stray piano detections are gone.
- Parenthesised backing vocals now get real timings too.

**How I checked it:**
- **Spoken lines:** aligned starts match where the voice actually starts, e.g. "None of it mine" was 2 s early before, now within 0.1 s.
- **Choruses:** all three independently land their lines on the same beats, to within half a beat.

> by the way: can we anti-alias everything? maybe we supersample it at 4k?

**Claude** <sub>17:34</sub>

Yes. The clean way is to **render everything internally at 2× (3840×2160) and scale down to 1080p for output**, which is 4× supersampling. The same switch can also produce a native 4K master if you ever want one. The drawing code doesn't need to change, because it all works in 1920×1080 coordinates and p5 handles the scaling. The one unknown is whether the paint library behaves at 2× density, so I'll test that before promising anything:

Supersampling is working and on by default for final renders (`9b8ec8f`).

- **How it works:** each frame is drawn at 3840×2160 and scaled down to 1080p, which is 4× supersampling. It smooths every style: outlines, paper-cut edges and hatching. The drawing code is unchanged, and line weights stay exactly the same.
- **Proof:** left is before, right is after, both zoomed 3×. Compare the black outlines round the chair spikes and the ear.

![before/after](out/demo/ssi_compare.png)

- **Cost:** 1.1–1.8× render time depending on the style. The full song goes from about 1.5 h to about 2.5 h on one worker, less with parallel workers.
- **Defaults:** full video renders use 2× automatically. Quick checks and contact sheets stay at 1× so the review loop stays fast. `--ss=3` or higher also works if you want it even smoother.
- **Native 4K:** also possible if you'd ever want to deliver in 4K rather than just render internally at it. That's a small extra change (the output canvas and the grain overlay); I'd leave it until the edit is locked.

One exception: the 0–2:20 subtitle clip already rendering was started at 1×. Future renders will be supersampled.

**Claude** <sub>17:40</sub>

Taking the direct notes first. Pair C is chosen, with less height difference, and the three-quarter profiles and noses get redone. I'm sending that back to the agent who designed them while we work through the style question.

All three questions have one answer, because the engraving's banknote feel is the useful part.

**What to do with the engraving: make it the look of money.** Banknotes are engravings, so anything that belongs to capital gets printed like currency:
- the bosses as portraits in ornate oval frames, like the monarch on a fiver
- the bunker, the share price and HR's door
- the bill itself: "You get the bunker; I get the bill" works as a pun, since a bill is also a banknote

I'd push it further towards real banknotes: swirling guilloche patterns and fine border work, printed in banknote inks (the purple of a £20, teal of a £5, red of a £50) rather than plain sepia. It shouldn't be tied to a time, because old money looks the same in every century, and that's the song's point: the rich have always kept the time that machines saved.

**Should the past change? No.** The rubber-hose ad is the *promise* ("Think of all the time you'll save"), and the intro and outro rhyme on it. It's historically apt too: Keynes predicted a 15-hour working week in 1930, the rubber-hose era exactly. Wherever the video needs real history, the banknote engraving can show it. The breakdown's "Don't tell me I hate the future" is the Luddite accusation, and that could be mills and machine-breakers printed like old money.

**What should the bad future be? A photocopy.** A copy of a copy of a copy:
- crushed blacks and halftone dots, toner grit and streaks, grey paper
- one sickly accent colour, like fluoro green or safety orange

It fits "muted colour, more grit" exactly, and it fits the lyrics: "Then it copied me, but cheaper", "Same shit, faster". The future is a degraded copy of now. It's also the medium of UK DIY flyers and rave and grime zines, which suits the track, and it's still hand-made, so it sits alongside the film cartoon, the cut paper and the pencil flipbook.

Two alternatives, if xerox doesn't grab you:
- **Blueprint:** the machines' plans. Cold cyan grids and dimension lines. Strong, but clean rather than gritty.
- **Glitch/CCTV:** the machines' eye. Cold and effective, but the only look that isn't hand-made.

**What this gets you:** each look means *whose world this is*, not just when.

| Look | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Rubber hose | the promise |
| Card | the two of them, the present |
| Banknote engraving | money and power |
| Photocopy | the decay |
| Flipbook | their hope |

Because the looks can switch per element, they can also share a frame. On the turbine street, his house could be photocopied while Musk's data centre looms over it, engraved like a banknote. That's the song's argument in one image.

**4K:** noted.
- **Native 4K output:** a small change to the engine, which I'll make before the final renders.
- **Supersampled 4K:** drawing each frame at 7680×4320. Your GPU can handle it, but it costs about 4× the current supersampled render: roughly 2 s a frame, about 4 hours for the song on one worker, less with parallel workers. Very doable.

**Pair C:** it's gone back to the designer to close the height gap and rebuild the three-quarter heads and noses properly (the head turns, rather than the cheek being sliced off, and the nose sits on the face).

**Next:** shall I concept these two looks the way we did the others?
- the bunker reprinted as banknote engraving, with Musk and Zuckerberg as note portraits
- the turbine street redone with his street photocopied under a banknote-engraved data centre

- **Colouring:** loose and scribbly over the lines (the current charm), or neater and denser inside the lines.
- **Paper:** notebook lines showing through, or plain sketchbook paper.
- **Wobble:** the redraw shimmer can go calmer or livelier.

Pick from those once you see it, or tell me in your own words what the first doodle version was missing.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>22:28</sub>

> I would also say the dot alignment is so bad that it makes the photocopied shots chaotic and hard to follow, and a bit visually jarring. otherwise doing well! there's a few bits that seem to be very short and cause almost double jumps

**Claude** <sub>22:30</sub>

Fixing the riso look now: I'm locking all the dot screens to one fixed grid on the screen, the way real print works, and cutting the misregistration way down.

Both issues are being fixed.

**The riso dots: fixed in the style itself.** There were three causes:
- **Dots didn't line up:** every piece had its own dot grid, and scaled characters had bigger or smaller dots than their surroundings. All inks now share **one grid locked to the screen**, like a real print, so neighbouring pieces' dots line up at any scale or camera move.
- **Misregistration:** it was 5 px between the colour screens. It's now 1.2 px, so there's just a hint of print rather than colour fringing.
- **Crawling dots:** the screens were shifted slightly every frame. Now they hold still.

Before (left) and after (right), same frame:

![riso](out/video/riso_compare.jpg)

The edges are crisp now, and a frame-by-frame strip confirms the dots no longer crawl. This applies to every riso shot automatically.

**The double jumps:** I measured the frame-to-frame change across the whole reel. About 25 spots have two big changes less than a second apart; the worst are around 0:36, 1:10, 1:21, 2:10, 2:36 and 3:35. They come from each chapter cutting precisely on the words, so a cut sometimes lands just before or after a transition. An editing agent is now going through each spot and fixing the rhythm: moving a cut to a better word, merging two quick changes into one, or extending a hold. No shot gets less than about 0.7 s between hard changes unless it's a deliberate hit on the beat. It will confirm each fix by measuring again.

The banknote legibility pass is still running too. Once both finish, I'll render a new full reel.

> all the characters are way to up/down jumpy all the time. lazy animation.

You're right, and the cause is systemic. The emotion system comes from the kit, where it was built for Clawd, a bouncy block mascot: every emotion's idle is a hop on every beat. Every character rig inherited it, so everyone in the video bobs up and down for five minutes whatever they're doing.

An animation-polish agent is fixing it at the source, in the character rigs, so it applies everywhere:
- **Humans (both narrators, and every caricature):**
  - no beat-locked bob at rest
  - instead, a living idle not tied to the beat: slow breathing, the occasional weight shift or head turn, and blinks, out of sync between characters
  - emotion still shows in face, pose and arms
  - reactions to a mood change stay, but small and crisp, sized for people rather than rubber balls
- **Bots:** they're cartoon mascots, so they keep some musicality, but the constant hopping goes. They get occasional accents on big hits and purposeful business instead: Grok's hands working, Muse's hover drift, Copilot's scarf. The rave still dances.
- **Deliberate actions stay:** the cannonball, jumps, startles and stamps. It's checking every place a chapter drives the characters' motion directly so none of those break.

**Claude** <sub>23:26</sub>

Two good catches. The pencil first: I'll angle it across the bun so the tip sticks out one side and the eraser end the other.

The pencil now goes through: tip out one side, eraser out the other, with the middle hidden in the bun. Now the card texture. Looking at how it's drawn:

Both are done and committed (`b28afbe`).

**The pencil** now goes across the bun: the tip pokes out one side and the eraser the other, with the middle hidden in the hair.

![pencil](/tmp/pencil2.jpg)

**The card texture:** yes, I get it, and you were right. It was screen-space in two ways:
1. The paint library's fill texture, which gave each card piece its mottle, is anchored to the canvas. So when a piece moved, it slid through a texture that stayed put.
2. The frame-wide paper grain is multiplied over the whole image in screen space.

The fix:
- **Each card piece now carries its own paper texture**, with fibres and grain mapped in the piece's own coordinates, so it moves, turns and scales with the piece and with the camera.
- **Each piece gets its own offset into the texture**, so neighbouring pieces don't share a pattern.
- **On card shots, the frame-wide grain is turned down** to 20%, just enough to keep the image from looking digital.

Checked with a moving card test: the grain matches when you follow the piece (difference 1.1) and doesn't match at a fixed screen spot (3.6). It's also slightly faster to render than before.

The turbine rework, the animation polish and the cut-rhythm pass are still running. They'll pick up this change automatically.

- **Noses:** for Zuckerberg, Altman and Dario (and to a lesser degree Dorsey and Turnbull), the nose is a small bump on the edge of the silhouette, low and detached from the eyes, so it reads like a pimple on the profile. A proper three-quarter nose starts between the eyes and runs out towards the turned side, with the tip about halfway between the eyes and mouth and the near nostril drawn inside the face.
- **Eyes:** Dorsey's and Dario's far eye (and Dario's far glasses lens) is the same size as the near one, so it doesn't turn with the face. The far eye should be narrower and sit closer to the nose, with both pupils looking the same way.
- **His eyes:** both are the same size, round and white, and the pupils point in slightly different directions, so he looks googly and startled even at rest. He needs correct near and far sizes, a matching gaze, and a calmer resting eye with the lid resting on the iris.

**The stamp skew (C1a, C2a).** The stamp squashes them in the hammock, but that squash was applied along their tipped-back bodies, not straight down, so it bent them into a diagonal parallelogram. Committed as `143ac7a`. I'm checking on the turbines render:

While the turbines render finishes, here's where things stand.

**Parallel rendering won't speed things up on this machine.** I built it and measured it:
- A single render already keeps the RX 9070 55–100% busy and uses 8–14 GB of its 16 GB of video memory, counting the other agents' renders.
- Several pages in one browser ran no faster, because Chromium sends all their graphics through one GPU process.
- Four separate processes were about 12× slower each, because video memory overflows and every frame waits on swapping.

The GPU is the limit, so I reverted the change. I also set the full-film frame renderer to one worker by default, since its old default of 4 risked the same overflow on 4K renders. The render is quicker than I first guessed: about 0.6 s a frame at 3× supersampling. A full-film 1080p render comes to roughly an hour and a quarter, less while the agents aren't rendering at the same time.

**The turbines clip:** 102.5–117.5 s, 1080p, 3× supersampled, with the song. It will be at `out/fixes/turbines_1080p_ss3.mp4` in a few minutes. Supersampled stills of the frames where riso and banknote meet are ready now in `out/fixes/turbines_stills/`:

**Claude** <sub>00:51</sub>

It still fails, even through the page's own render function. So the likely cause is the environment, not my profiler. Checking whether the normal renderer works right now:

`render.mjs` renders fine, so the fault is in my standalone launcher. Rather than debug it, I'll add the profiler to `render.mjs` itself as a `--profile` mode, which is worth keeping anyway:

The clip is good: 15 s at 1080p with the song. Now I'm starting an agent on the supersampled-4K slowdown, with a strict rule that the look can't change:

Your turbines clip is ready:

**`out/fixes/turbines_1080p_ss3.mp4`**: 102.5–117.5 s at 1080p, 3× supersampled, with the song. It starts just before the copier sweep and ends as the exhaust prints the frame black into V2g. Supersampled full-resolution stills of four frames where riso and banknote share the screen are in `out/fixes/turbines_stills/`.

**On render time, supersampled 4K will take hours, not weeks.** I added a profiler (`render.mjs --profile`) that splits each frame into drawing, GPU, compositing, encoding and transfer. Over 20 frames sampled across the film:

| Mode | Per frame (mean) | Whole film |
|---|---|---|
| 4K native (3840 px drawn) | 0.49 s | ≈ 1 hour |
| 4K supersampled 2× (7680 px drawn, scaled down) | 2.5 s | ≈ 5 hours |

Most of the supersampled cost is one fixable problem:
- **A GPU cliff:** at 3840 px the GPU takes 1–65 ms a frame; at 7680 px it takes 0.6–4.8 s. That's up to 190× more time for 4× the pixels, so something runs out of video memory at that size. It hits the card frames hardest: the office, HR, the box room and the train.
- **Some drawing code also grows with resolution:** on some frames the JavaScript part rises from about 0.2 s to 1.3 s when it should stay flat.
- **Encoding and transfer out of the browser are small** (about 0.1 s), and parallel rendering can't help on one GPU.

I've started an agent (renderopt) to find what's allocated at 7680 px and fix it. One thing it'll test is rendering the supersampled frame in 3840-px tiles so the GPU never goes over the cliff. The look can't change: every change gets checked against before/after 4K stills with a numeric diff and crops, across all five looks. It will also time 1.5× supersampling as a middle ground.

If the cliff goes away, supersampled 4K should come down to roughly 1.5–2 hours for the whole film. Until the agent reports, treat that as an estimate.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>01:13</sub>

> also, I'm not sure the "AGI on a stick" in the car on the side-by-side shot really is coming through. I wonder what else could be on the stick? a gpu? inference? what would that even look like? what could be used?

**Claude** <sub>01:14</sub>

First, the shot as it is now:

The orb doesn't read, for three reasons:
- **It's a copy of the sun.** It's the same guilloche rosette as the sun in the top left corner, so it looks like a second sun.
- **The stick reads as an aerial.** It's a thin vertical line off the car, and nothing shows the car is chasing the thing hanging from it.
- **The car is a dark hatched lump.** It reads more like a loaf than a sports car.

Options for what hangs off the stick:

1. **The AI sparkle ✦:** the four-pointed star every AI product now uses as its button. An engraved gold one, twinkling, is today's shorthand for "AI". It's probably the fastest read for a general audience, and it pops on "A-G-I".
2. **The brain from C1b:** a pocket-sized version of the cathedral brain from "All that intelligence". It's a callback: "AGI" is the big brain they keep promising. It's strong if people remember the chorus.
3. **A GPU:** a graphics card with two cooling fans that spin like the plant's fan, and gold contacts. It reads as "compute" to tech-literate viewers, and it's nicely literal: they're chasing hardware. Less universal.
4. **Inference as the typing dots:** a chat bubble with the three "…" dots pulsing forever, a thought that never arrives. Everyone knows the typing indicator. It says "the model is thinking" rather than "AGI", though.
5. **A literal carrot:** the donkey gag says "chasing something you'll never catch" instantly, and the lyric supplies "AGI". But it's the least specific to AI.

Whatever hangs off it, the staging needs fixing as well:
- The stick should be a rod clamped to the bonnet, arcing forward over the windscreen, with the prize dangling just out of the driver's reach.
- The bros lean in, and one grabs at it.
- The prize stays exactly one arm's length ahead as the car bounces.
- The sun rosette gets replaced so nothing competes with the prize.
- The car gets a sports-car silhouette.

My pick is **the sparkle**, with **the brain** a close second. I'll mock up the sparkle, the brain, the GPU and the typing dots side by side under a switch (like the plant one), so you can choose by looking:

- **`out/video/reel_1080p.mp4`**: the same film scaled down from the 4K, for easy viewing (812 MB).

Frames sampled from the 4K file itself:

![](out/video/reel_4k_frames.png)

## What happened overnight
**Rendering is 2.5× faster at supersampled 4K.** renderopt found the cause: p5.brush paints every fill through a hidden canvas the size of the whole drawing. At 7680 px, each of about 400 fills per frame grabbed about 1.3 GB of fresh video memory, and the GPU thrashed. Two small fixes in `src/core.js` brought a frame from 2.4 s to 0.95 s. With that fixed, two render processes now help as well, so the whole film rendered in 1 h 11 min plus a 6-minute encode. The pixels are unchanged: before and after differ no more than two runs of the old code differ from each other (`out/renderopt/parity_before_after_ss4.png`).

**The night train is packed.** Every seat not in the action has a bot passenger, three to a seat in C2. The racks are full, bots perch on the window sills and stand between the seats. They're rim-lit violet-grey silhouettes of the cast's shapes with soft eyes, so the featured bots still read first. When the reservation cards pop, the passengers under them look up and give a little hop. Every train shot has it, down to F4 and Z2.

![](out/crowds/train2/compare_t140_90.jpg)

**The AGI chase in V2g is restaged** as a carrot on a stick: a red engraved roadster with a fishing rod on the bonnet, the prize hanging at Musk's eye level, and Musk lunging for it on the beat and missing. There are four prizes to choose from, and the reel uses the sparkle:

![](out/agistick/sheet_lefthalf.jpg)

**The 4K cast set is complete:** 15 characters in all 5 looks, including the 8 banknote images that failed last time. I also fixed the lineup page catching people mid-blink (Dario's eyes were shut). Sheets are in `out/cast4k/sheets/`.

A full-film sweep found no errors, and everything is committed; the last commit is `cec1a81`.

It anti-aliases fill edges very slightly differently, a difference far smaller than video compression's own loss (`out/renderopt/option_msaa_is_slow.png`). Now that a full render takes about 1.3 hours, I'd leave it off and keep the output exact.

**Claude** <sub>09:23</sub>

Both crowds stay: the V2d platform commuters and the V3a grandstand. Two agents are now on the banknote work.

**How real banknotes make the portrait stand out.** Several techniques work together:
- **Two printing layers:** the main figure is deep-engraved in the darkest ink. Everything behind it is a pale, mechanical underprint of fine ruled lines, guilloche and lathe patterns.
- **Different line language:** the figure's lines curve with the form (contour hatching, cross-hatched shadows, tiny dots for skin on faces). The background's lines are straight or evenly waving machine rules.
- **A vignette halo:** the background tone fades to bare paper in a soft oval around the portrait.
- **Value:** the figure spans deepest black to paper white. The background stays in a narrow, light band.

**What's running:**
- **bankelab:**
  - Building that figure/ground system for the banknote look. While a character draws, it gets the dark ink, contour hatching and dotted skin with a paper halo; backgrounds and props drop to the pale underprint. It applies automatically to every banknote shot and comes with a switch, so you'll see before/after on the bunker, Dorsey, the roadster and the race.
  - Also making each banknote scene more ornate: frames and cartouches, rosettes, fluted columns, fine engraved detail. The order is the bunker (it's used three times), the brain (whose folds currently look like a contour map), Dorsey, the roadster, the island and the stamp.
- **horserace:**
  - V3a's horses as elegant clockwork thoroughbreds, like a banknote vignette: arched necks, fine legs, streaming manes, engraved muscle, delicate rivets and gears.
  - An ornate frame around the race.
  - The speed pushed hard: a flying gallop with all four hooves off the ground, speed lines, the rail whipping past, turf flung up. Sam Altman is lifted flat off the saddle like a flag in the wind, clinging on by the reins, terrified.

Both notes are logged in the directive. I'll show you before/after images and clips as each one finishes. After that, the fresh 4K reel.

**The worn poster (done, `11e678d`).** As the camera pulls back, the poster shows its years on the wall:
- sun-yellowed, most at the top
- creases from being folded in quarters

It fades in during the pull-back, so the cut from the ad still matches.

![](/tmp/wear2/both.png)

**The card jitter (with the new jitter agent).** I measured it: the card look's stop-motion "nudge" (every figure re-placed by up to a pixel or two, 12 times a second) is only about a fifth of it, so there are other sources. The agent is ranking each source on a truly still figure and removing them. I've given it your rule for the card look as the goal:
- **Rooted:** a piece nobody touches stays pixel-identical, the same card reused frame to frame.
- **Rigid:** parts move by rotating and sliding on their joints like a paper puppet, with very little squash and stretch. Breathing becomes a slight rise of the shoulders and head.
- **Replacement pieces:** optionally, mouths and eyes swap between a few held shapes instead of morphing.

**V3c, "picking every lock":** you're right, houses don't work. Taken with the line before it, "I click nine little buses just to prove I'm not a bot, while yours pick every lock on every door we've got", the joke is the bot check: the test built to keep bots out stops *him*, and the bots sail straight through. The rest of the verse escalates the same idea: they cheat on the homework (V3d), get into his health portal (V3e), get root (V3f). So the doors should be the gates of online life. Houses make it read as burglary, which also fights the sincere-bots rule.

**My recommendation: a high street of the services we rely on.** A bank, a GP surgery, a school, the council, a corner shop. Each shopfront's door is locked with a website's bot check:
- an "I'm not a robot" checkbox
- a captcha grid
- a password padlock
- a two-factor code keypad
- a "No bots" sign

The Codex pets, sincerely out on an errand, tick each box and stroll in, one door per beat, with a tick popping each time. He's still stuck at the first door, clicking buses. It keeps the captcha-to-door morph out of V3b, and the school, surgery and bank set up the next three scenes.

Alternatives:
- **Websites as doors:** browser windows with login screens, paywalls and cookie walls. Clearer as "the internet", but it loses the street, and the film lives in physical places.
- **A server corridor:** racks with padlocks. It reads as "cybersecurity", but it's abstract, and V3f's "root" already goes there.

Shall I build the high street? I'd start once horserace is out of `verse3.js`, so the two don't collide.

  - a bank (coin sign), with a two-factor code keypad on the door

The card jitter is fixed. A figure standing still in the card look is now pixel-identical from frame to frame, the same piece of card reused as you described.

![](out/jitter/crops/bed_her_face_4k.png)

The top two rows are before: her face, and in pink everything that changed since the previous frame. Before, every frame changed. After (the bottom two rows), most frames are completely still, and the only changes are the deliberate breathing steps.

**What caused it**, measured on a still test figure:
1. **The simulated stop-motion "nudge" (about 97% of it):** every figure was re-placed by up to a pixel or so, 12 times a second. Now a figure gets a new offset only when it actually moves, then stays put.
2. **Breathing and weight shifts:** they crept by fractions of a pixel each step, which read as flicker rather than breath. They now hold still and move in clean half-pixel steps (a full pixel at 4K), with the breath slightly deeper so each step reads.
3. **Squash and stretch:** slumps and emotion reactions used to squash whole card figures. Now the figures are rigid: her and him keep their legs planted while the shoulders, arms and head rise or drop together, and the other characters no longer squash in card. Clawd's legs no longer stretch.
4. **The card texture:** it now moves with its piece instead of sliding across it.

Every other look is unchanged. One side effect: every card piece's fibre pattern sits at a slightly different offset now, a one-off change you'd only notice comparing old and new frames directly.

Side-by-side clips, before left and after right, are in `out/jitter/clips/sbs/`. They cover a close-up of her, the platform, the office, the box room and the train.

- The optional swap to a few held mouth and eye shapes wasn't done.

A new agent (horse2) is rebuilding the race from the ground up, with your gears idea as the design.

**1. Research.** It'll collect banknote horse vignettes: 19th-century American Bank Note Company engravings, US currency vignettes, racing certificates, and modern notes with horses (Mongolia, Kazakhstan, Scotland). It'll also study how engravers model form: lines that swell and follow the muscle, cross-hatched shadows, lines breaking to bare paper in the lights. It'll write the rules it finds as a short style guide and save the references in `out/horse2/ref/`.

**2. A real gallop.** The janky anatomy came from hand-built shapes. This time the horse comes from Eadweard Muybridge's 1878 "The Horse in Motion" photographs, which are public domain. It traces each frame's silhouette, so the proportions are a real horse's and the gallop is the real cycle, with the rider included.

**3. Your clockwork design.** Each horse is an elegant engraved thoroughbred shell, with windows cut into the barrel, shoulder and quarters. Through them you see a dense mechanism turning with the stride: gears of every size, a flywheel, cranks and connecting rods driving the legs, pistons at the joints, chains and sprockets. It's like a cutaway plate in a Victorian engineering book, or a watch movement seen through a skeleton case, and it spins faster when the horse bolts.

**4. Riders in the same style.** The jockeys' bodies (silks, crouch, boots, arms to the reins) come from the traced Muybridge rider and are engraved exactly like the horses. Only the caricature heads come from the rigs, drawn like banknote portraits. Altman's bolt is redrawn as one connected figure, streaming horizontally from a single thread.

You'll get a model sheet first (the horse standing and galloping, with a rider, at 4K), then the shot. If there are two good ways to engrave it, it'll show both.

Fixing the pence first. My digit loop drew three digits after the point instead of two:

**The pence is fixed:** every price now has exactly two pence digits (£771.12, £222.21). My drawing loop was counting the decimal point from the wrong end, which put an extra digit after it.

![](/tmp/bills3/crop.png)

Which bill do you want in the film, the receipt or the final demand? I'd go with the receipt.

**The blotchy border:** it's the soft oval of bare paper behind each character. It's a paper-coloured gradient laid over the background lines, so where it's half-transparent it half-erases them into smudges. Real banknotes do it with the lines themselves: each background line tapers and ends as it nears the portrait, the ends staggered along a soft oval, so the edge is clean. A new agent, banktrack, is rebuilding the halo that way.

**How the lines should move:** it's also testing your question, because on moving subjects it matters. There are four options:
- **Bound to the item:** the engraving travels rigidly with each piece, like the card texture.
- **Screen-locked:** the lines stay fixed to the screen and figures slide under them, like the riso dots.
- **Redrawn:** the lines re-draw several times a second, like the doodle's wobble.
- **A mix:** for example, characters bound to themselves while backgrounds stay locked.

It'll render the same moving moments in each mode side by side, with the song: Dorsey riding up the share line, the bouncing roadster, the stamp swinging in, and a camera move in the bunker. Then it'll recommend whichever is easiest to follow by eye. You'll get the clips to judge for yourself.

**Claude** <sub>10:54</sub>

To your two questions: here's what each moment is meant to say, and why it doesn't land.

**The train chorus.** The train is the film's frame for her real, daily life, and each chorus line is meant as one image on it:
1. **"Fully automated – taking the piss!"** The night train is driverless and run by bots, and every seat is reserved for bots. A helpful bot offers her a seat, a "reserved" card pops up, and she's left standing at the pole. The point: automation serves the bots, not the people who ride.
2. **"All that intelligence, and we get this?"** She sends up a request, a cathedral-sized brain works enormously hard on it, and it sends down a sad meal-deal sandwich. The point: huge capability, trivial payoff for ordinary people.
3. **"You get the bunker; I get the bill."** The train passes the billionaires' bunker, and their bill flies in through the window onto her table. The point: they feast, she pays.

It doesn't cohere because the three beats use three unrelated mechanisms (a seat, a food order, a bill) with nothing connecting them. The fix I'd suggest is one thread: **the train's at-seat service, run by bots.**
- The steward bot tries to seat her, but every seat's card shows a bot face, so she stands.
- She presses the service call button to order, and the order goes up to the brain, which sends back the sandwich.
- Then the steward brings the bill for it on a little tray. When she looks at it, the camera follows where the bill came from, out through the window to the bunker's feast. Her bill includes their banquet.

It becomes one little story: no seat, a terrible sandwich, and a bill that pays for their party.

**The horse race on the club wall.** It's a billboard outside the bots' club advertising "the race", with a boss shouting through a megaphone. She mimics him ("If we don't, then China will!"), and the camera pushes into the billboard and the race comes alive. Nothing explains why a race ad is on a club wall.

My suggestion: **a bookmaker's shop across the street.** British bookies have race screens in the window, and "we're all in" is betting talk. Sheltering from the rain, the two of them watch the race build-up on the screens, with the boss shouting before the race. The camera pushes into a screen, and the race plays out. The next shot (V3b, his bus shelter) would pull back from a screen or his phone instead of the billboard.

**Claude** <sub>10:55</sub>

**The train:** a new agent (trainchorus) is removing the seat gag entirely: her trying to sit, the reservation cards, the domino, and the two of them on the luggage rack in the second chorus. The new "Fully automated – taking the piss!":
- **The train at work:** the packed night train run entirely by cheerful bots doing every job people used to do. A trolley bot serves, a ticket bot punches tickets for bot passengers, a cleaner bot wipes the window, the doors open themselves, and the departure board flips. It ripples along the carriage on the beat.
- **Her reaction:** she stands at the pole, like any commuter, and gives us a deadpan look on "taking the piss".
- **Into the next beat:** she presses the train's service call button and sends her order up. That leads straight into the brain, the sad sandwich, the bunker and the bill, so the section runs: her order, what the "intelligence" gives her, and who really gets paid.

The second chorus is the same, busier, with both of them squashed standing among the bot passengers.

**The race:** it moves to a bookmaker's across the street, with race screens and odds boards in the window, and "we're all in" becomes betting talk. That's queued until horse2 finishes rebuilding the race in the same code, so the two don't collide.

## Where the film stands
- **Coverage:** all 54 shots are animated across the full 4:59, in all five looks, with subtitles.
- **Rendering:** a supersampled 4K render of the whole film now takes about 50 minutes, down from an estimated 5 hours. The last full reel is from 4 a.m. (`out/video/reel_4k_ss4.mp4`). About 40 commits have landed since, so it's well out of date.

## Done since that reel (all committed)
**Look and feel**
- **Card:** figures stay still when standing (no more jitter) and move rigidly like paper puppets. Faces and glasses cut clean at 4K.
- **Banknote:** denser, finer engraving; characters stand out from the paler backgrounds; ornate borders, rosettes and architecture.
- **Faces and likenesses:** fixed 3/4 noses and eyes, and redrawn Musk, Altman and Zuckerberg from reference photos.
- **Riso and crowds:** the turbine section is all riso, silhouette crowds are in the public shots, and the night train is packed with bots.

**Story and clarity**
- **Intro:** the ad freezes into a worn, gritty poster, holds, then the camera pulls back.
- **Verse 2:** the V2c thought bubble has time to float; the do-not-disturb phone reads; the AGI chase is a carrot on a stick with a brain as the prize.
- **Chorus:** the brain's sandwich round trip is clear.
- **Verse 3:** V3c is the online high street; the V3d pupils break in through the door.
- **Bills:** the restaurant receipt first, then a flood of utility bills (electric, gas, internet, water, council tax, phone), then the AI services bill she folds into the plane.
- **Fixes:** the stamp no longer skews the figures; the Gemini twins' hands are clasped properly.

## In progress (5 agents)
| Agent | What |
|---|---|
| horse2 | V3a racehorses rebuilt from Muybridge's gallop: clockwork horses with visible gears, riders in the same engraving style, Altman losing grip progressively |
| banktrack | cleaner banknote halos; testing which way engraving lines should move so they're easiest to follow |
| banquet | the feast grows: small in chorus 1, bigger in chorus 2, grand in the finale |
| trainchorus | the train chorus without the seat gag: a bot-run train, then her order and the sandwich |
| z1chase | bots chasing them through the four styles, changing at the panel borders, piling up at the good-future line |

**Queued:** the race moves to a bookies' window once horse2 is out of that code.

## Small open items
- **"we've got" in V3c:** it's sung just after the cut into V3d. Holding on him through it means moving that cut about half a second.
- **Musk, Zuckerberg and the bosses:** they still breathe with a tiny whole-body stretch in card. Making it a rigid shoulder rise is a small rig change each.
- **Riso bills:** the bills' thin lines print blotchy in riso and need tuning.
- **Race:** the horse and saddle rough edges will likely be superseded by horse2.
- **Optional:** mouths and eyes could swap between a few held shapes, like real cut-out animation.

## Next, as I see it
1. **Land this batch.** As each agent finishes: review, commit, and show you before/afters.
2. **Integration pass.** Four agents share `chorus.js` and `final.js` right now, so a full-film error sweep and cut-by-cut check once they're done.
3. **A fresh supersampled 4K reel** (about an hour) for you to watch straight through. Watching end to end tends to surface pacing and story issues that shot-by-shot review misses.
4. **A punch list from that viewing,** then final polish and deliverables: the 4K master and a 1080p version, plus any other formats you want.

The main risk right now is several agents editing the same few files at once. I'm checkpointing after each one lands so nothing is lost.

Banktrack has finished both banknote questions, and both changes are now on by default and committed.

**1. The blotchy border is gone.** The paper-coloured wash is replaced by what real notes do: the background lines themselves taper to a point and stop around each character, their ends staggered along a slightly wandering oval, so the figure sits in clean bare paper. The agent also tried a crisp paper cut-out (middle), but it looked like a sticker and chopped the patterns with a hard oval.

![](out/banktrack/halo/bunker_73.0_compare.jpg)

**2. How the lines should move:** it tested your four options, plus a couple of variants, on moving subjects, and measured how much the lines crawl across each object:
- **Screen-locked (like the riso dots):** the lines swim over everything that moves, and under camera moves.
- **Redrawn (like the doodle):** strobes, and makes the still background flicker too.
- **The mix won:** characters and objects carry their own engraved lines with them, and the background stays fixed to the world. It halved the crawl on the bouncing roadster, the worst case before, and was as steady as the old behaviour everywhere else.

In this strip the roadster is held still: each row is five frames of one method, and the right-hand column shows where the lines move (darker means more crawl). The third row is the one now in use:

![](out/banktrack/still_strip_v2g.jpg)

The racehorses are rebuilt and already in the film, and the next step (the bookies' window) has started.

![](out/horse2/04_one_horse_big_4k.png)

**The horses:**
- **Shape:** each horse is traced from Muybridge's 1878 "Sallie Gardner at a Gallop" photographs, so the proportions and the gallop cycle are a real horse's.
- **Machinery:** windows are cut in the shoulder, barrel and quarters, and the mechanism inside turns with the stride and spins faster on the bolt. There's a train of spoked gears, a chain, a governor whose weights fly out with speed, a watch balance wheel, and a crank driving rods into the legs.
- **Engraving:** each mass of the body has its own family of lines (bands across the neck, lines round the ribs, arcs round the hip), and where the families meet they read as panel seams on a made machine. A simpler engraving that just follows the outline is still available behind a switch; it looked like a contour map.
- **Inks:** the lead horse is in the darkest ink, and the pack in steel, brass and copper.

**The riders** are engraved in the same line as their horses: silks, breeches, boots, stirrups and reins. Only the heads come from the caricatures, set on top like banknote portraits.

**Altman loses the horse bit by bit:**

![](out/horse2/02_cast_sheet_runners_and_altman_stages_4k.png)

1. **"A race":** proud, megaphone up.
2. **"to build a thing":** the horse surges, the megaphone tumbles away, and he hauls on the reins.
3. **"we might not know":** he's thrown out of the stirrups and clings on with both hands.
4. **"how to":** one hand slips and he streams out like a flag.

The shot cuts just after "how". "Rein in" plays on the bus-shelter screen in V3b, where he's down to a single thread.

Up close at 4K, though, the traced outline is lumpy in places (the head and the legs), because it comes from a small 1878 photograph. Dorsey's shredder is finished and committed (`35c57f1`). The riso review reel is still rendering; the first clip is done.

**The shredder (V1e)**

![](out/shredder/v1e_machine_4k_100pct.png)

**The machine** is engraved cast iron on lion's-paw feet: a big flared hopper, toothed steel rollers, brass gears, a mainspring barrel with a butterfly winding key, and a belted flywheel with a patterned rim. It also has a copper chimney that puffs, a pressure gauge with a red zone, and an output chute. The rollers and gears turn with the feed.

**How it plays:**
1. **The cut in:** the paper chain falling out of the previous shot lands in a queue of paper workers holding hands, winding back to the horizon in rows, so it reads as thousands.
2. **Before "Jack":** Dorsey twists the winding key twice and lets go on "Jack". The machine clunks, puffs and shudders, and the gauge needle jumps.
3. **"cuts four thousand":** he steps back, turns away and reads his phone, one hand in his pocket. The machine pulls in one worker per bite, then keeps running on its own, faster. Paper strips and scraps (a head, a tie) spray from the chute and blow away.
4. **"watch the share price":** the scraps shoot up in a column, and the green share-price line grows out of the pile.
5. **"soar":** he rides the line up and out of frame, still looking at his phone.

![](out/shredder/v1e_keybeats.jpg)

To watch it with the song: `out/shredder/v1e_clip_28-33.6.mp4`.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>14:07</sub>

> why is the camera movement sometimes looking really laggy / low fps?

**Claude** <sub>14:08</sub>

Most likely it's the stop-motion timing. The card and doodle looks animate on twos (12 new pictures a second, each held for two frames), and some shots compute their camera moves from that same stepped clock, so a pan or zoom only moves every other frame. The draft can tell me which shots do this: I'll measure every frame against the one before it.

The lag comes from the stop-motion timing. In the card and doodle looks, everything animates on twos: 12 new pictures a second, each held for two frames of the 24 fps film. That's right for the characters; it's the stop-motion feel. But many shots compute their camera moves from that same stepped clock, so pans, pushes and slow drifts only move every other frame too, and that reads as laggy.

I measured the draft to confirm it: frame by frame, I found 13 stretches where the whole picture jumps on alternate frames:
- **Her office:** 15–30 s.
- **Her flat:** 37–43 s.
- **The notebook opening:** 52–58 s.
- **The finale (252–292 s), the worst:** the train, the four panels, the notebook and the outro.

Even the office's tiny breathing zoom steps at 12 fps, which makes the whole frame judder.

A new agent, camsmooth, is fixing it with the rule real stop-motion uses (motion-control camera): **puppets on twos, camera on ones**. Characters and card pieces keep holding for two frames, but every camera move gets computed from continuous time, so it moves smoothly every frame. That covers pans, pushes, pull-backs, drifts, shakes, and the roadster's scrolling landscape.

It'll turn my measurement into a reusable check, fix each shot, re-measure, and give you before/after clips of the worst stretches. It won't touch the platform scene while platform2 is still working there. It's logged in the directive.

Running now: **camsmooth** (the camera fix) and **platform2** (the roomier platform scene). The second draft render goes once both land.

> I think fine is good. I also want us to look at the ink/line art, because it looks okay but doesn't really fit how a printer would print

**Claude** <sub>16:34</sub>

Setting "fine" as the default dot screen, then briefing an agent on the riso line art:

The fine dot screen is now the default across the film (`ba7a896`). The old coarse one is still available as `?risodots=coarse`.

**The line art:** you're right. The riso outlines are currently a clean comic-ink brush stroke drawn over the print, so they look like a pen drawing laid on top rather than something that came out of the machine. A new agent, risolines, is studying how line art actually prints and building two or three treatments behind a switch (`?risolines=`), with the current lines kept for comparison:
- **Riso stencil line:** the line printed solid from one ink drum as its own layer, so it's offset from the colour layers by one consistent amount per print. It has tiny voids and uneven ink density, slightly soft stencil edges, and uniform weight (no brush swell or taper).
- **Photocopier line:** toner-black, slightly ragged, with toner scatter and specks along the edges.
- **A combination** of the two.

It's also checking three things:
- **The copies degrading:** lines should thicken, break up or drop out as the copies degrade, since that's how generation loss looks.
- **Line weight:** whether the lines are now too heavy against the finer dots.
- **No shimmer:** the texture has to be locked to the screen like the dots, so it doesn't crawl or flicker on held drawings.

You'll get 4K crops side by side (a face, a set, a copy generation), 1080p frames and a moving clip per treatment, plus its recommendation.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>16:38</sub>

> I think part of the problem is that the riso effect isn't proportional to screen size. it looks quite good at a goldilocks amount, but then sizing it too small removes the look, and making it too big makes it look like artistic halftone. that's the problem, perhaps

**Claude** <sub>16:38</sub>

Yes, I think that's it. Two ratios matter here:
1. **Dot size against the frame:** fixed. The dots are measured in screen pixels, so 1080p and 4K look the same, just sharper.
2. **Dot size against the subject:** this one varies, and it's the real problem. A fixed screen means:
   - **Wide shots:** a 300-px character gets about 40 dots top to bottom with the old screen, so it reads as a bold artistic halftone.
   - **Close-ups:** the 4K cast stills blew each character up to about 800 px tall, so they got the goldilocks look of about 100 dots top to bottom.
   - **The floor:** pushing the dots much below ~3 px at 1080p makes them dissolve into flat colour.

So the fix is to size the dots to the shot:
- **Per shot:** each riso shot sets its dot pitch so the main subject lands near that goldilocks density.
- **Clamped:** the pitch never goes below the size where dots dissolve or above the size where they turn graphic.
- **One screen per shot:** the pitch stays fixed within a shot, so the dots still line up across every piece and don't crawl.
- **Printed objects:** for things that are printed in the story (the pinned copy, the posters, the commute copies), the dots belong to the paper, so they'd grow as the camera pushes in, as they would if you really filmed a print.

It's all in the riso code, so I'm adding it to the risolines agent's job rather than starting a second agent in the same file:

I've added this to the risolines agent's job:
1. **Measure the target:** it'll count how many dots span a character's height in the 4K cast stills you liked, and use that density as the target.
2. **Find the limits by eye:** the floor where dots dissolve into flat colour at 1080p, and the ceiling where they turn graphic.
3. **Build it** as a new mode (`?risodots=auto`): each riso shot gets a pitch that puts its main subject at the target density, clamped between those limits. Printed objects keep dots that scale with the camera.

You'll see the current "fine" screen and the automatic one side by side, in wide shots (the high street's small figures), close shots (her at the bookies) and the copies, at both 1080p and 4K. Then you decide whether it becomes the default. It's logged in the directive.

**The line art: three treatments.** Here's her at the bookies at 1080p:

![](out/risolines/1080/lines_crop_bookies.png)

- **What was wrong with the old line:** it's a comic brush stroke, swelling to twice its width and tapering. It redraws 12 times a second, so still drawings shimmer. And its navy isn't an ink the riso has: the press prints only blue, fluoro pink and yellow.
- **What all three new versions share:**
  - Even weight, as a printer reproduces a line.
  - The texture is locked to the page, so held drawings stay perfectly still.
  - The colour under the line is cleared, and the line prints on top, as a real separation does.
- **a, riso blue:** the line from the blue drum only, with a hairline of bare paper where the plate sits off. It's the purest, but too light at 1080p: figures lose their edges.
- **b, photocopier:** black toner with ragged edges and specks. The most legible, but black isn't one of the riso's inks.
- **c, riso copied (recommended):** blue and pink overprinted into a deep violet, close to the old navy, with a thread of pink along one edge. It reads well at 1080p using only the press's own inks.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:34</sub>

> we also have at least one situation of characters appearing in wrong style for their current setting. e.g. in the beta test scene where he's an acting pawn, the bg is photocopy and he isn't. or gemini on his health app. We need to be consistent in why these are justified, or fix it

**Claude** <sub>17:35</sub>

Agreed: some of these were deliberate, but the film never makes the reason visible, so they read as mistakes. The storyboard intended him to be "the only card thing" on the riso film set, the one real person among the production's printed extras, but on screen he just looks unconverted.

A rule that justifies the good cases and catches the rest:
1. **A figure takes the medium of the place it's physically in.** In the riso world you're riso; at the banquet you're banknote.
2. **Pictures inside pictures keep their own medium, bounded by their frame:** a screen, a poster, a page, a phone. The race on the bookies' TV is this case, and it's getting the photocopy pass.
3. **Crossing between media happens only at a visible boundary or moment.** The bill flying from the vault into the train, the bots turning from riso to card as their feet leave the phone's screen, the chase changing at the panel dividers. The change is shown, never just assumed.

Under this rule, his film-set moment becomes a crossing: on the clapperboard's CLAP he gets printed along with the set, reduced to one of the extras in their beta test. That fits the lyric better than him staying card. I'll have an agent audit the whole film for mismatches:

A new agent, styleaudit, is applying that rule across the whole film:
- **The film set (V3e):** the CLAP becomes the crossing. On the clap he's printed along with the set, a copier-style flash, and becomes one of the riso extras in their beta test.
- **The Gemini twins and his health app:** it'll work out whether the app's screen is a frame. If the twins hop into the app, they change medium as they cross its edge, like the bots leaving the phone.
- **The rest of the film:** it goes through the film shot by shot and makes a table of every figure in a different medium from its setting. Each one either gets fixed (usually redrawn in the setting's medium, or given a visible crossing) or listed as justified, with the reason, so you can see why it's allowed.

It won't touch shots other agents are mid-way through (the race TV, the heist, the finale, the platform); it'll list anything it finds there for me to route. I've logged the rule in the directive so it stays consistent from here on.

- **Limbs, hands and feet:** thin solid-black hose limbs with no outline, big white puffy gloves, big oval shoes.

I've updated `brief/animation-style.md` with every rule we've settled (`0cebfd4`). Your original three-sentence draft stays at the top, verbatim, and the rules follow in six sections:
1. **One medium per world:** the table of the five looks, plus the three-part media rule (figures match their setting, pictures in frames keep their own medium, crossings are shown).
2. **Motion:** puppets on twos with the camera on ones, card that's rigid and reused with replacement mouths, no beat bob, the banknote background never redrawing, no double jumps, finished transitions.
3. **Each look in detail:** the rubber hose idiom (with your Codey reference), card's crispness, the doodle rules, riso's auto dots and printed lines, and the banknote's elaboration, figure/ground and line-based halos.
4. **The cast:** her, him, the bots (sincere, no hats), the Codex pets with Codey leading, and the caricatures (pointed, not cruel).
5. **Craft rules:** no words, clipped frames, draw order, pointing, props that travel, attention before reactions, crowds, subtitles.
6. **Output:** the 4K supersampled master and the 1080p drafts.

It says the dated directive log wins if the two ever disagree, and the brief's README now links it, the polish checklist and your reference images. I'll keep it current as new rules come in, and the polish round's agents will work from it.
