- **In one line:** a 1930s ad promised machines would give us our time back; two young Brits find the machines everywhere and the time nowhere, and what they wanted was never the robot butler but a Friday together.
- **The two of them:** who they are, what happens to each, and that they're strangers on the same commute who become friends.
- **The world's devices:**
  - the train as their lived experience, her notebook as imagined futures
  - the five media
  - the bots are sincere (the harm comes from how they're deployed and who profits, not malice)
  - the bosses as caricatures in the banknote world
- **How it moves:** section by section, from the ad through both verses, the choruses, the rave, the bridge and the final chorus, to the outro, which sets the ad beside the Friday.
- **The through-lines:**
  - the promise against the reality
  - her notebook's three drawings
  - the bills
  - the paper planes (the CV that dives into her notebook, the bill she throws back)
  - the platform and the poster
  - the good future having no visible machines

**Why it fails:**
- **It doesn't read as a reverse angle.** V2d is meant to turn the camera round to face the tracks, but the far wall is a copy of the bench wall, with the same tiles, blue band and poster. It looks as if the car appeared in front of the bench, on a track that wasn't there.
- **Nothing tells us the camera turned.** He stays on the left facing right, exactly as before.
- **Continuity breaks.** The bench, the rope and the card terminal all vanish.
- **The eye roll lands on a second copy of the poster,** not the intro's.

- The camera stays facing the bench wall, and the car pulls in across the foreground, where the intro's train was.
- The bench, the rope and the one poster stay where they are.
- The car stops with its open rear balcony near the middle of the frame so it never hides him. Clawd is on the balcony, facing him, with a sad little wave, and the premium passengers board.
- The car pulls out the way it came, which also matches the window light sliding in V2c.
- He's left alone with the poster behind him: his eyes follow the car out, then go to the poster with a push-in, then the eye roll, at the timings you approved.
- The current version stays behind `?v2d=reverse` for comparison.

**One thing decides how much work the audio is:** whether the clean track is the current master with the words replaced, or a newly generated song. `brief/audio-style.md` says the song came from a generation prompt.
- **If it's the current master with the words replaced,** only those 25 words need aligning.

**Claude** <sub>19:22</sub>

Yes, an agent is now restyling the subtitle banner so it matches each world. My starting ideas for it, which it's free to improve:

| Look | Banner |
|---|---|
| **Rubber hose** | A 1930s sing-along with a bouncing ball hopping word to word (the Fleischer "Screen Songs" were that era's karaoke), period lettering on a title-card strip, pastel colours and film grain |
| **Card** | A strip of real cut card with a lift shadow; as each word is sung, a cut-paper chip in the singer's colour slides behind it, on twos |
| **Doodle** | A torn scrap of lined notebook paper, with the words handwritten in biro and written on or coloured in as they're sung |
| **Riso** | A riso-printed slip in the blue ink, with the sung words overprinted in fluoro pink for Tess or yellow for Rowan, slightly misregistered |
| **Banknote** | An engraved ribbon with a guilloche border and engraved serif capitals |

A few rules I've set for it:
- The banner changes style at the same moment the picture changes medium, including partway through a line that runs across a cut.
- Tess stays tomato and Rowan stays mustard in every style.
- Every word still fills exactly as it's sung.
- It has to stay readable at 1080p.

**Claude** <sub>20:19</sub>

Yes for Altman, definitely. For the gent and the butler, yes too, but carefully, and as an option you switch on rather than a replacement. I've started an agent on all three.

**Altman is the clearest mismatch.** In V3d he's still the detailed card caricature: eye whites with irises, lids, fine hair, shading, just with the ink outline and gloves added. He shares the frame with the pets, who are now properly in the idiom, for 14 s, so it reads as two drawing styles in one frame. Cartoons of the time already had a model for this: Disney's "Mickey's Gala Premier" (1933) drew Hollywood stars in full rubber hose. Altman will get:
- pie-cut eyes, simple round shapes, hose limbs, puffy gloves and big shoes
- his recognisable features kept and exaggerated: the big round head on a slight frame, the messy hair, the wide eyes and brows, the gown and mortarboard
- his V3d acting kept: the arm up then out toward the exit, the marking

That becomes the default, with the old Altman behind `?altrh=0`.

**The gent and the butler are closer already, but not all the way.** The gent reads as 1930s, but his limbs are tailored sleeves and trousers rather than hose. The butler is more a retro robot than a rubber hose character: stiff trousers, pupils in his goggles. Each will get hose limbs, pie-cut eyes, gloves and shoes, and a bit of bounce. They keep the gent's boater, blazer and moustache, and the butler's brass box body, grille grin, tray and cocktail.

**Why the ad is only an option for now:** it's the piece you approved at the start, and it runs through the whole film:
- the intro and the outro
- the platform poster
- her riso copies
- the burning ad
- the robot butler chasing them in Z1

It's also the reference palette for the pastel look. So `?adrh=1` will switch the new versions on everywhere at once, while the default stays pixel-identical to the ad you approved until you've compared.

One rule stays in place: the rubber hose idiom has a minstrel-caricature history, which is why Tess and Rowan keep their own features and bare hands in ink. White gloves stay on the bots, the tech bosses, and these two white 1930s cartoon figures.

You'll get before/after sheets for Altman in V3d and for the ad in every place it appears, plus a V3d clip. In the meantime I'll carry on toward the polish round.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>20:26</sub>

> Do Tess and Rowan need to match too?

**Claude** <sub>20:27</sub>

I'd say no, at least not all the way. They should match the rubber hose world's drawing medium, and they already do. They shouldn't take on its character features.

**The idiom's signature features come from minstrelsy.** Pie-cut eyes, white gloves, and the black-faced construction with big white mouths are all inherited from minstrel performance; Fleischer's Bosko was explicitly a blackface character. Tess and Rowan are both characters of colour. Putting those features on them would reproduce exactly that caricature, which is why the directive from 2026-09-24 keeps them in their own features in ink: their eyes with whites, their own skin tones, bare hands. Altman, the gent and the butler don't carry that risk, so the full treatment is fine for them.

**They already match as far as the medium goes.** They only appear in the rubber hose style for about a second, running through the first panel of the Z1 chase. There they already get:
- the ink outline, in proportion to their size, with lighter inner lines (from the rubber hose rethink)
- the pastel palette (from today's pastel pass)
- the projected-film grain and weave

So they read as "drawn in that world" without being turned into its caricature. They're also visitors from the present passing through the ad's world, so them staying recognisably themselves works for the story too.

**One optional extra:** a 1930s bounce in their run for that panel, a springier stride with squash on each footfall. That's pure motion, with none of the risky features. It's small, and I'd only add it if the panel feels stiff next to the new Altman and butler when you see them together.

> can you render the wide shot bunker scene in 720p, 1080p, 1080p ss, 4k, and 4k ss

**Claude** <sub>20:40</sub>

The widest bunker shot is C2c at 147.6 s: the long banquet with all the bosses and the galleries, which has the most fine engraving. Rendering it at each resolution.

All five stills are rendered, and they turned up something important for delivery. The shot is the wide banquet in the second chorus (C2c, 147.6 s), the one with the most fine engraving.

Full-frame PNGs are in `out/res_bunker/`:
- `bunker_720p.png`
- `bunker_1080p.png`
- `bunker_1080p_ss3.png`
- `bunker_4k.png`
- `bunker_4k_ss4.png`

The film can't render natively below 1080p, so the 720p is the 4K master shrunk down, which is how a 720p copy would be made. Here's the same detail from each, blown up to the same size so you can see the pixels:

![crops](out/res_bunker/crops_compared.jpg)

**The engraving isn't the same drawing at each resolution.** It's drawn for the resolution it's rendered at:
- **At 1080p** it uses fewer, bolder lines that hold up at that size.
- **At 4K** it switches to the finer detail you asked for: roughly twice as many lines per head.
- **Supersampling** keeps the same lines and smooths their edges: compare the jaggy 1080p with the draft 1080p, supersampled 3×.

**The catch:** anyone watching the 4K master at a lower resolution gets the 4K's fine lines squeezed into too few pixels, and that causes moiré:

![delivery](out/res_bunker/delivery_compared.jpg)

- **720p shrunk from the 4K master** (top left): the crosshatching on the jackets and hair turns into checkerboard patterns.
- **1080p shrunk from the 4K master** (bottom left): holds up better, but the hair and jackets are starting to shimmer.
- **The copies made from a 1080p render** (right): stay clean at both sizes.

In motion that moiré will crawl, which is worse than in a still.

This matters because YouTube (or any site) builds its 1080p and 720p versions by shrinking whatever you upload. It can't use separate renders per resolution. The options:
- **Upload the 4K master** (the usual choice): full detail for 4K viewers, and YouTube gives 4K uploads a higher bitrate at every size. Lower-resolution viewers get some moiré in the densest engraving.
- **Upload a 1080p master:** clean at 1080p and 720p, but no 4K detail.
- **Tune the 4K engraving to shrink gracefully:** keep the 4K detail but space the finest crosshatching a little wider, so it survives being shrunk. I'd lean this way.

Before deciding, I'd render a short moving bank shot from the 4K master, shrink it to 1080p and 720p, and see how bad the crawl actually is. Want me to?

**UncleHerbert** <sub>20:49</sub>

> what will the scene-by-scene polish round entail?

**Claude** <sub>20:50</sub>

The polish round is a systematic pass over all 54 shots for craft problems, not new ideas. Nothing locked gets touched: the Z4 page flip and its timing, the stamp, the V2d eye-roll timing, the ad as you approved it. Here's the plan.

**How it's split:** one agent per chapter, about seven in all:
- intro and outro
- verse 1
- the two choruses
- verse 2
- the break and verse 3
- the breakdown and bridge
- the final chorus

Only three or four run at a time, so they don't fight over the GPU. The V3d, intro and outro agents wait until the rubber hose agent has finished with Altman and the ad.

**What each agent checks, shot by shot,** against the checklist in `brief/polish_checklist.md`:
- **Staging and continuity:**
  - hands and props drawn in front of what they should be in front of
  - props going where the characters go, and not popping in or out
  - arms pointing where the character means
  - eyelines landing on what they react to
  - a character visibly noticing something before reacting
- **Media and framing:**
  - the media rule
  - pictures inside pictures clipped to their frames
  - no hats hiding designs
  - no words in pictures
- **Motion:**
  - puppets on twos, camera on ones
  - card rigid and still when untouched
  - the engraved background not redrawing
  - no two big changes within about half a second
  - every transition finished, with no empty or washed-out frames
- **Artefacts:** stray marks, visible seams and clip edges. New this round: the new subtitle banners are different heights in each look, so each agent checks the banner never covers the key action.
- **Characters:** costumes, the bots staying sincere, the caricatures on model.

**Known leftovers to fix:**
- Z2: the paper plane stays card when it flies into the engraved bunker.
- Z3: the task bubbles aren't clipped to the phone.
- V3a: the race on the bookies' back-wall screen is riso, while the window TV's is engraved.
- V2h: the life card is drawn twice for a moment (130.26–130.45 s).
- Grok's beret, under the hats rule.
- V1g: the second of near-black at the rainy doorway.
- "If we don't, then China will!": the lyric data puts it in Rowan's voice, but the storyboard has Tess saying it. That affects the subtitle colour and who lip-syncs it.

**One proposal for you to confirm first:** apply the office's lip-sync logic to the other quoted lines. The characters being quoted could mouth their own words, the way the boss now does:
- "Mine says 'You're a great fit.' Theirs says 'No, you're not.'": her Muse and their Gemini
- "The oracle says 'I hear you.'": Clawd
- "If we don't, then China will!": the boss on the bookies' screen, or Tess mimicking him

The agents would only list candidates; nothing changes without your say.

**How it's run:**
- Clear defects get fixed directly.
- Anything creative goes behind a switch or onto a questions list.
- Each agent commits its own chapter, with before/after sheets for every fix.
- I review each chapter's before/after images, then bring you one list covering what changed, the questions, and the decisions still open:
  - V3c's cut timing
  - the V3d briefing's reverse-shot length
  - Z3 A or B
  - the turbine street's dot size changing at each cut
  - Codey's gloves
  - the three deliberate media crossings

**Then:** the full 1080p draft of the whole film, for a fresh viewing.

Anything you'd like added or left out before I start?

The polish round has started, with both of your additions built in.

**Pacing, now part of the checklist and checked in every shot:**
- **Too much in too little time:** each agent lists every beat in the shot with its time and judges whether a first-time viewer can read it. The rough rule: a simple action needs about half a second, and a new idea or gag needs about a second. Where beats pile up, they re-time them within the shot, simplify, or move a minor beat somewhere quieter. Anything they'd cut goes behind a switch and comes to you as a question.
- **Periods of inaction:** each agent measures frame-to-frame motion above the subtitles, flags any second or more where nothing moves, then judges by eye. True dead air gets resolved. The holds you asked for stay, but must have life in them:
  - the poster hold
  - the V2d linger
  - the Z1 pile-up
  - the breakdown's stillness
  - F2's breathing room

**Lip-sync:** quoted lines go to whoever is being quoted when they're on screen: Muse says "You're a great fit", Gemini "No, you're not", Clawd "I hear you". Tess keeps "If we don't, then China will!" with her rolled-up flyer as a megaphone.

**Waiting:** intro/outro, the break and verse 3, and the final chorus start once the rubber hose agent has finished Altman, the gent and the butler, since they share those scenes.

"Posing and movement" is a new checklist step, run on every held pose and key drawing:
- **Awkward poses:** each pose must read clearly as a silhouette, with weight on something. The agents are hunting for:
  - elbows or wrists bent the wrong way
  - limbs passing through bodies or props
  - arms hanging dead through an emotional line
  - both arms, or two characters, moving in mirror image
  - feet floating or sliding
  - hands that don't actually land on the pole, the seat or the card
  - a character facing away from who they're talking to
- **Moves that suit the character:**
  - Tess is dry and economical.
  - Rowan is grounded and heavier on his feet.
  - The bots are eager and bouncy on their accents.
  - The bosses are smug and expansive.
  - Nobody fidgets or freezes without a reason.
- **Moves that suit the style:**
  - Card pieces rotate rigidly on their joints, with stop-motion holds between moves.
  - Rubber hose is bouncy and stretchy.
  - Doodle is loose and hand-drawn.
  - Banknote is stately.

Fixes will be re-posing, re-timing, or adding the missing wind-up, settle or follow-through, each with a before/after.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>23:20</sub>

> we need animation to transform each subtitle style in a way that still allows easy tracking of the text. because they jump suddenly currently, it's difficult when there's scene changes to follow the text when the size and positioning changes.

**Claude** <sub>23:22</sub>

The subtitle transitions are with the agent that built the banners. There are two parts to the fix.

**Less to change:**
- **Line breaks:** each line will split into rows the same way in every style. At the moment a line can be one row in one style and two in another, and that's the biggest jump.
- **Position:** the text will sit on the same baseline, height and centre in every style.
- **Words:** each word holds its place as closely as the fonts allow.
- **Highlighting:** sung words stay lit through the change.

**A quick transform instead of a cut:**
- Over about a quarter of a second, the banner's shape tweens from the old style to the new.
- The text crossfades between the two fonts in place, and any word that has to move slides rather than jumps.
- Where the picture changes with a sweep (the copier's light bar, a wipe, the tear), the banner's change follows that sweep across.
- It stays fast enough to keep up with Z1's quick panel changes.

You'll get every-frame strips across several style changes, plus clips of the poster tear into the platform and of the Z1 chase.

**F4 is also done:**

![F4](out/polish/bridge/f4_a4_after.jpg)

- **The pen:** she holds the biro in a writing grip, her hand resting on the page as if she's just finished the drawing.
- **The sketchbook:** it's now A4, a little narrower than her shoulders.
- **Her arms:** her forearms lie over the book with her elbows by her sides.
- **The slam:** her pen hand lifts clear as the cover shuts, then comes back down on it, pen still in hand.

One size note, now in the style brief: the characters' cartoon hands are small, so a hand-based measurement doesn't work in the wide shots. The sketchbook is now sized against her phone and her shoulders, and the hand only in close-ups drawn at life size, like the outro.

**Claude** <sub>23:26</sub>

It was meant to be his building cut open like a doll's house, showing his box room inside, with Clawd's tree plugged into the whole street. As drawn, it doesn't read that way:

![V3f](out/check/v3f_room.jpg)

The room is a clean card picture in a black frame sitting on the front of the building. There's no cut wall and no flats around it, and it's in a different style from the riso street. So it reads as a TV or billboard floating there, which is exactly what you saw.

Rather than make it a billboard, I'm making it the cutaway it was meant to be, because that tells the story: "I can't get a room" while he's stuck in a tiny room inside a street the bot now runs. The verse 3 agent is on it:
- **A real cutaway:** the front wall broken open around his room, with a rough edge, the wall's thickness, the floor and ceiling slabs, and the neighbouring flats above, below and beside it. No black frame.
- **The same medium as its surroundings:** his room is printed in riso like the rest of the street, since it's part of the building, not a picture on it.
- **A proper crossing into the breakdown:** as the camera pushes into his room (the bed drops, the crumb lands), the copier's light bar sweeps across and his room comes out as card. That's the film's established riso-to-card device, and the breakdown opens on that card room as it does now.

All the beats and timings stay: the tree, the roots, the crown's burst, the bed and the crumb.

If you'd rather it be a billboard after all, say so. It would get the photocopy pass like the bookies' TV.

The F4 pose came back better: her arm no longer covers the drawing. At 4K, though, I think it still isn't right, so I've sent it back before you had to point it out.

![F4 4K](out/polish/bridge/f4_hold3_after_4k.jpg)

- **The pen arm** comes in from the wrong side and runs flat under the book, parallel to her other forearm. Two stacked horizontal red arms read as stiff and contorted.
- **The pen** isn't really held: the barrel sticks out away from her fingers, gripped only at the tip.

The fix is simpler: she's finished drawing, so the pen is put away. It goes into her bun next to her pencil (the pencil through her bun is part of her design, so it's in character), or clipped on the spiral. Then:
- her far hand holds the book's right edge from its own side, with her red cuff showing;
- her near hand keeps the spine;
- both hands sit at the book's two sides, and nothing crosses the page.

The slam stays simple too: the far hand lets go as the cover swings shut, then takes the shut book's edge again.

Thanks, 24–26 are logged. All three were already how the film plays, so nothing changes.

**Z3, A or B.** This is the "Your bots escaped the sandbox; I can't escape the rent" moment, 4:35–4:48. Both versions share the setup:
- Her AI bill lands beside her notebook on the train table.
- Her phone lies flat, showing the bots digging in their riso sandbox.
- On "Your bots…", each bot gets a task and climbs out of the phone onto the table, turning from riso to card as its feet leave the screen.
- Her page shows her drawing of the two of them.

Then they split. The later frames, the rent book and the odometer, are the same in both.

**A, the sand wall (the current default):**

![A](out/z3ab/z3_a.jpg)

- The bots' task is her page. Pleased to help, they build a crenellated sand wall right round the drawn couple, and Codey plants a flag.
- On "I can't escape the rent", the wall squeezes in on them like her box room's walls, and the landlord taps the rent book.
- They stay walled in through the odometer.
- Before "I count the life I've spent", Rowan's hand brushes the wall off the page and they spring apart. Then the page flip.

**B, the page's edge:**

![B](out/z3ab/z3_b.jpg)

- The bots hurry off the table to their tasks.
- The drawn couple let go of each other's hands, run after them and hit the edge of the paper, pressed flat to it like mimes on glass (Z1's pile-up turned round). Then they trudge back.
- On "rent" they're back in place, hand in hand, and the landlord taps the rent book. There's no wall, so Rowan's hand goes straight to turning the pages.

**What each one says:**
- **A:** the bots' eager help literally walls them in. It ties up the sandbox and sandcastle thread from both choruses and her box room's closing walls. It also gives Rowan one small act of pushing back just before his line. It's the busier of the two.
- **B:** the lyric's contrast in one image: the bots get out, the people can't get off the page. It's simpler and sadder, but the couple's run is small and quick at this size, and there's no payoff before his line.

**My recommendation is A:** it has the stronger through-line and the brush-off payoff. You'll see A playing in the draft. Once the draft finishes I can render a short clip of B too, if you'd like to compare them in motion.

Understood. Z3 goes back to the writers' room, and the page flip stays exactly where it is, at its exact timing.

**The brief for this stretch:**
- **The span:** 4:34.9 to about 4:39.7, from her AI bill landing on the train table to the odometer.
- **The lines:**
  - "(Same shit, faster!)" at 4:35.6
  - "Your bots escaped the sandbox;" at 4:36.6
  - "I can't escape the rent." at 4:38.3
- **Where it has to land:** on the notebook, so Rowan's hand can take the pages on "I count the life I've spent". It should hand the meaning to the flip: this is how she wants to live, time spent with a friend.

**Three pitches:**

**1. The rent book on the page.** The simplest, and my pick.
- The bots climb out of her phone and scatter happily off the table to their tasks. Her phone is left empty: they escaped.
- On "I can't escape the rent", the landlord's hand drops the red rent book flat onto her open notebook, right over the drawing of the two of them.
- It sits there through the odometer ("You keep a count of hours saved"), with the bosses' count on one side and her dream buried under the rent book on the other.
- On "I count the life I've spent", Rowan's hand slides the rent book off the page and starts turning the pages: the flip, at its exact timing.
- **Why it works:** it's one clear image. His pushing the rent aside to count the life they've spent becomes the lead-in to the flip, and it takes the place the wall's brush-off had, so the flip's timing doesn't move.

**2. The doors.**
- The train stops at a station in the gleaming city from F4. The doors slide open and the bots pour out onto the platform, off to their tasks.
- Tess and Rowan stand up to follow, but the doors close in their faces. A rent book is pressed against the glass from outside.
- They sit back down to the notebook on the table, and the flip.
- **Why it works:** it ties up F4's "still waiting outside" and the train as their lived experience.
- **The cost:** more new staging, and it's busier in 4.8 s.

**3. Faces at the table.**
- We've been on hands and props for a while, so this pulls back to the two of them at the table.
- The bots climb out of her phone and run off along the table and out through the window's gap. They watch them go.
- On "rent", the landlord's hand plonks the rent book on the table between them. They look at it, then at each other.
- Rowan pushes it aside and opens her notebook. The camera goes in to the page, and on to the odometer and the flip.
- **Why it works:** it's the most human, a shared look before the memories.
- **The cost:** the camera has to travel from wide to the flip's close framing.

Pitch 1 and pitch 3 could also be combined: 3's shared look, with 1's rent book landing on the page.

One question back: does the odometer stay as it is between "rent" and the flip? I've assumed yes, since you picked it earlier.

Pick one, mix them, or throw them all out and we'll keep going.

**Their count on her phone, face down.**

- **One continuous place,** with no cut away to a split screen.

**Claude** <sub>09:22</sub>

Yes, I know what you mean. Where a shape overlaps itself, the fill cancels out in the overlap, like an even-odd fill rule. I found it in both places:
- **The Dorsey share-price line:** at every zigzag corner the ribbon folds over itself, and the overlap shows as a notch with no hatching.
- **The doodle Friday:** where Tess's arm crosses her jumper, the background's blue crayon shows through as if cut out.

**Arms and elbows:** an agent is on it now.
- **Overlaps:** it will fix the fill so overlaps stay filled in every style, at the root in the shared engine code. Then it will hunt for any other places this was happening.
- **Elbows:** the arm solver will pick the elbow's bend side from anatomy, with the upper arm angled out from the body and the forearm folding in front. It won't flip mid-move, so Rowan's typing elbow can't flicker in-out-in.
- **Your cases:** V2h's typing, the doodle Friday, and "That was the point!", where her upper arm should point outward and the palm-up motion stays as it is.
- **The doodle Friday:** Tess and Rowan will be re-posed so their arms don't cross messily and have some life through that long stretch.

**The riso dirt:** you're right that it reads as film. Every frame, the riso shots scatter about 260 fresh specks at random, flicker a few vertical streaks and wobble dark edge bands. That's projector dust and scratches, not a photocopier. A second agent is replacing them with a copier's own marks:
- **Fixed marks:** the same streaks and specks in the same place on every copy, steady through a shot, because a copier's drum leaves the same marks each time.
- **New prints:** specks only change when the print changes, as with each new copy in C1d.
- **Steady marks:** a steady platen-edge shadow, and a little uneven ink.
- **Rubber hose:** keeps its film grain, dust and scratches exactly as they are.

I've added both rules to the checklist too.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>11:54</sub>

> almost afraid to ask; you know the bouncing ball on the subtitles for the old style? it currently lands approx in the first 1/4 of the word's total width. Should it:
>
> a) land in the middle instead
> b) jump on each syllable on the word
> c) keep the same

**Claude** <sub>11:55</sub>

I'd go with **b, a bounce on each syllable**. That's what the real Fleischer "Screen Songs" did: the ball hit every syllable as it was sung, so multi-syllable words got several small hops ("in-tel-li-gence" = four). It's what makes the bouncing ball feel musical rather than a pointer. Landing in the middle (a) would be tidier than now, but it's still one hop per word, so long words would feel sluggish against the vocal.

- **Timing:** we only have word timings, not syllable timings. But the lip-sync already splits each word into sounds, with the vowels timed inside the word, so each syllable's bounce can land on its vowel onset. That puts the ball on the sung beat without new audio analysis.
- **Position:** each bounce lands over the middle of that syllable's letters. Syllables are split with a simple English rule (vowel groups), so "au-to-ma-ted".
- **Pace:** fast lines get quick little hops, and a held word gets one bounce and a rest, like the originals. Very short syllables would merge into a single hop, so it never gets jittery.

It's only in the rubber hose banner (the intro, V3d's classroom, the Z1 ad panel and the outro's frozen ad), so it's a small change. Want me to go ahead?

**UncleHerbert** <sub>11:57</sub>

> yes

**Claude** <sub>11:59</sub>

Built the per-syllable bounce. Now checking which words it splits and how it looks.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>11:59</sub>

> the paper's lines in the credits at the end is very very feint and the contrast almost looks blown out

**Claude** <sub>12:04</sub>

The ball now bounces on each syllable, and it's committed. It lands over the middle of each syllable's letters, timed to when that syllable is sung:
- "se-cret" gets two hops.
- "or-gan-ised" gets three.
- "Op-en-AI" gets three.
- Single-syllable words like "Think of all the time you'll save" still get one hop each.
- A held word gets its hops early, then the ball rests while the note holds.
- Syllables closer than 0.12 s merge into one hop, so fast words don't jitter.

**How it splits words:** a simple English syllable rule. It handles silent e's ("love-ly", "re-place-ment", "home-work"), keeps sound pairs together ("ma-chines", "no-thing"), leaves blends with the following syllable ("ta-ble", "de-clined"), and treats spelled letters as one hop each ("A-G-I"). For timing it reuses the lip-sync's sound timing within each word. Where that doesn't line up with the syllables, it spaces them evenly and lets the last one hold.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>12:06</sub>

> spoke too soon! office didn't get split up into two syllables

**Claude** <sub>12:08</sub>

Fixed and committed: "office" now gets two hops, "of" then "fice".

![office](out/check/office.jpg)

The ball was landing on each syllable's first consonant, and in a quick word like "office" the "ff" starts only 0.09 s after the "o". The two hops were closer than the 0.12 s anti-jitter limit, so they merged into one. It now lands on each syllable's vowel, which is where the sung beat actually falls, so "fice" lands 0.155 s later and keeps its own hop. Every multi-syllable word in the film now gets its full set of hops, except a few sung faster than a quarter of a second, where two hops would jitter:
- "never", "Elon's" and "taking" get one hop.
- "A-G-I" gets two.
- "elaborate" gets three.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>12:08</sub>

> and effort

**UncleHerbert** <sub>12:08</sub>

> and didn't

**UncleHerbert** <sub>12:10</sub>

> If it's intended it's fine, I get there's an anti-jitter limit. Just not sure how it was done historically

**Claude** <sub>12:14</sub>

"Effort" and "didn't" both get two hops now, and it's committed:
- **"effort":** it was already splitting after the office fix. The clip you watched was rendered before that fix went in.
- **"didn't":** it was being treated like "don't", one syllable. After a consonant, "n't" is sung as its own syllable, so it's now "did-n't", "was-n't", "could-n't". "don't", "can't" and "aren't" stay one hop.
- **Hop height:** quick hops have a higher minimum now, so they read as bounces rather than a slide.

**On the historical question:** as far as I know, the Fleischer bouncing ball hit every sung syllable. In the early 1920s Song Car-Tunes it was, I believe, a real white ball on a stick, moved by hand in front of the lyric card in time to the music. That's why its bounces vary: big arcs between slow words, tiny quick skips on fast syllables. It never skipped a syllable to avoid jitter. So I've matched that:
- A fast syllable now gets a quick low skip instead of being merged.
- Only landings less than two frames apart would merge, and nothing in the film is that fast.
- Every multi-syllable word now gets its full set of hops, including "taking" in the Z1 chase's rubber hose panel.

- `out/check/ball_z1_taking.mp4` (Z1)

My guess about the riso lines showing through was wrong. The agent checked, and a shape's fill does hide the lines under it: the coat's side line stops where the arm crosses it. So there's no wider riso problem. The real cause was the pose:
- **Too close:** he stood so near the lock that his arm folded double, which made the zigzag notch at the elbow.
- **Wrong layer:** it's his far arm in the three-quarter view, but it was drawn over his chest, which left sleeve lines stopping in the middle of the coat.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>16:48</sub>

> also the scroll on the credits seems very juddery

**Claude** <sub>16:48</sub>

The judder and the shimmer have the same cause. The scroll moves about 3.9 pixels a frame, which doesn't divide evenly. Rounded to whole pixels, the steps go 3, 4, 4, 4, 3…, and that uneven rhythm reads as judder.

The fix is the one broadcast rolling credits use: a whole number of pixels per frame, the same every frame. That's 4 px per frame at 1080p (96 px/s), which becomes exactly 8 px per frame in the 4K master:
- **Every frame steps by exactly the same amount**, so the scroll is perfectly smooth.
- **The ruled lines land on the same pixel position every frame**, so combined with the bolder, crisp rules they can't shimmer.
- **The page is refitted to that constant speed**, so the Friday drawing still lands for the ending hold and the prints keep their time on screen.
- **Short, whole-pixel ramps** replace the ease in and out at the start and end.

It's also checking that the scroll moves on every frame, not on twos. The report will list the scroll offset frame by frame at both 1080p and 4K, to prove the steps are constant, along with a short clip with the audio.

The rave's shadows now stop at the foot of the wall. Before on the left, after on the right:

![arches](out/fixes/v3/b1_arches_before_left_after_right.jpg)

The dark bands turned out to be the server-stack pillars along the side walls, not the arches. Each pillar's blue riso tint ran down to the pillar's own foot, which for the near pillars is well below where the wall meets the floor, so the band spilled over the tiles. The engraved drawing underneath was already correct; only the riso tint on top was bleeding. It's now clipped at the wall-floor line in every interior shot, including the whip-pans, the push-in and the view through the window. Committed.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:08</sub>

> the tips of the spikes of the sun, on the poster, go weirdly right-angle-spikey when the photocopy filter is applied

**Claude** <sub>17:09</sub>

I can see it: on her riso copy of the ad, each of the sun's rays ends in a little right-angled hook, like a "7", instead of a clean point. The original rubber hose ad draws them fine.

![sun](out/check/sun_riso.jpg)

It's almost certainly how the riso line code handles very sharp corners. When a line is widened, the corner at an acute tip overshoots and folds back, which makes the hook. The verse 3 agent is fixing it in the shared riso line code, so sharp tips end in a clean point or a slightly rounded tip. Because the code is shared, it'll also check other riso shapes with sharp corners: stars and sparkles, the burn's flames, the turbine flames, V3c's pictogram signs, and Z1's riso panel.

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:09</sub>

> lines are way better, still somehow looks juddery though

**UncleHerbert** <sub>17:11</sub>

> sorry, I need to provide more context with these notes. the *credits* lines are way better, but the credits still looks a bit juddery on the video you sent. Did that have super sampling?

**Claude** <sub>17:11</sub>

Yes, both clips were supersampled: the 5 s scroll clip at 3×, and the full credits clip at 2×, the default for clips. Supersampling smooths edges within each frame, though, not motion between frames, so it isn't the cause.

I measured the 5 s clip frame by frame. The text, both columns of prints and the page all move exactly 4 px on every frame, so there's no stutter in the render itself. What you're seeing is 24 fps strobing: sharp, un-blurred text scrolling at 24 fps reads as juddery. It's worse on a 60 Hz screen, which shows alternate frames for 3 and 2 refreshes, so the motion lurches. Cinema credits have the same problem; film hides it with motion blur from the camera's shutter.

The fix is in progress: a shutter motion blur on the scroll. Each frame blends the page over half of its 4 px step (2 px at 1080p, 4 px at 4K). It only applies while scrolling, so still frames and the holds stay sharp. The agent will send before/after clips. If it softens the handwriting too much, a lighter blur is the fallback.

**One question from the Z3 fixes.** To show the bots actually crossing the phone's edge (riso over the screen, card beyond it), the camera now lands on Tess and her phone for the climb-out, and only widens to include Rowan as they run for the window (about 277.25–277.8).

![Z3](out/fixes/final/r2_z3bots_after_strip.jpg)

The catch is that Rowan's surprised reaction as they come out is now off-frame. The alternative is a quick push in on the phone from the wide, but that would be two camera moves back to back. I'd keep it as is. OK?

The sun's rays are fixed and committed. Before on the left, after on the right:

![sun](out/fixes/v3/riso_corners_sun86_before_after.jpg)

- **The cause:** each ray has a short flat tip between two sharp corners. The riso line is one continuous stroke with pointed corners, and at such a sharp turn the corner shot back past the tip's length, folding the stroke over itself into that little "7".
- **The fix:** at very sharp corners only, the riso line now breaks into pieces with rounded ends, so each ray ends in a clean, slightly rounded tip. Gentle corners and curves are untouched.
- **Checked at 4K:** the sun on the wall copy and on the burning copy, plus other riso line work: C2f's flames, V3c's shopfronts, Grok, Z1's riso panel and V2e. All the same or better.

**The gallery guests' lines:** that's the engraving's lighting. The light comes from the upper left, and engravers leave the lit tops of round forms, like heads and shoulders, as bare paper. The lines stop in a fairly hard arc rather than tapering off, which is why it reads as missing lines. If you'd like, they can taper out instead.

Each sheet stands up and the copier's light bar prints the copy in from the feet up. ![print-in](out/fixes/v1/fix22_V1d_print_in_first.jpg) The four cheaper copies at 28.1–28.3 s were cluttered by their overlapping blank sheets, so they're getting narrower sheets sized to each copy.

My quick check images are drawn at 1x with no supersampling, so they alias fine lines; the examples you saw were probably those. The agent is also checking that the supersampling really averages each pixel's area when it scales down. If it only samples a few points per pixel instead, thin lines break into dashes in the final renders too. I'll bring you the videos to judge by eye.

> nice work. how's our git state looking? have we been keeping it all checked in and clean?

**Claude** <sub>18:57</sub>

Git itself is clean. `out/` is 50 GB on disk though, so a quick look at what's in it:

Yes, git is clean: everything is committed and nothing is stashed.

- **Working tree:** clean on `main`, 516 commits. Each change went in as its own commit with a message saying what changed and why.
- **Nothing lost or half-done:** no stashes, no stray branches, and `git fsck` reports no problems. The pre-commit hook that rejects files that don't parse is in place.
- **Backups:** two tags, `backup/ad-old-gent-butler` and `backup/pre-switch-cleanup`. Older versions are recoverable from history anyway, as today's switch back to Tess's gesture D showed.
- **Worktrees:** the two extra ones in /tmp belong to the agents still running (the "productivity boom" restage and the bank note tests). They remove them when they finish.

Three things worth your attention:

1. **The project exists only on this disk.** The only remote, `upstream`, is the framework's repo (JohnHeibel/ClaudeAnimationBase); we've never pushed anywhere. A private GitHub repo, or a `git bundle` copied to another drive, would protect about 500 commits of work. Tell me which you'd prefer.
2. **`upstream` would accept a push.** A stray `git push` would try to send our work to the framework author's repo. I can make that remote fetch-only with one command.
3. **`out/` is 50 GB of renders.** Git ignores it, so it isn't a git problem, just disk space. The biggest folders are old draft videos (12 GB), the frame cache for the full-film render (5.8 GB, rebuilt on each draft), and experiment folders (`polish`, `editor2`, `risolines`, `renderopt` and so on). Every design you've picked is already saved in `brief/ref/history/`. Given the lost early designs last time, I won't delete anything until you've seen the list; I can put together a list of what's safe to remove and what to keep, such as the cover deliverables and the latest draft.

The bank note line tests are done. I recommend option D: of the four, it's the only one whose ground lines never break up, at 1080p or at 4K.

**Why they were breaking up:**
1. **The check images you saw.** They were my quick sheets, drawn at 1× with no supersampling, where the thin lines come out as dashes. That exaggerated it.
2. **1080p supersampling wasn't averaging properly.** At 3× down to 1080p, Chrome's image scaling doesn't average each pixel's full area, so fine lines beaded, and the beads crawl when the camera moves. That's fixed and committed: each 1080p pixel is now the exact average of the 3×3 block drawn for it. The 4K master was already exact, and the extra cost is negligible.
3. **The pen itself.** The brush library draws each line from dabs that swell, thin and occasionally drop out, so the lines print slightly broken even in the 4K master. Only D gets rid of this.

**The four options:** each figure is the share of each line's length that prints as a visible gap (lower is better).

| | 1080p | 4K |
|---|---|---|
| A: current | 4.5% | 5.8% |
| B: minimum line width | 4.2% | 8.2% (widened pale lines turn grey) |
| C: coarser, heavier lines | 2.9% | 1.6% (reads as woodcut) |
| **D: solid engraved lines** | **0%** | **0%** |

![1080p crops](out/banklines/crops_1080_c2c_wall.png)
![4K crops](out/banklines/crops_4k_c2c_table.png)

**About D:**
- **How it's drawn:** as solid shapes, the way the figures' outlines already are. No line is ever thinner than one pixel; where the tone needs lighter, the line gets paler instead. The lines swell slightly in the shadows and taper into the figures' vignettes, like real banknote engraving, and it renders faster.
- **It's paler than now.** You recently asked for stronger ground lines, so you may want D darker; that's one setting.
- **It loses the hand-inked grain** in the background. That fits the look we agreed: figures deeply engraved over a paler, mechanical underprint.
- **The rose window's dense centre goes flat** at 1080p. At 4K its detail shows.

**Best viewed as videos, in motion.** Each shows all four side by side at 100%, with the song, in `out/banklines/`:
- `compare_1080_c2_2x2.mp4`: the chorus 2 cathedral, the carriage and the banquet push-in
- `compare_1080_c1_2x2.mp4`: the same for chorus 1
- `compare_1080_v1e_2x2.mp4`: the Dorsey shot
- `compare_4k_c2_2x2_100pct.mp4` and `compare_4k_c1_2x2_100pct.mp4`: the 4K versions

The agent also found and fixed a real bug that turned the chorus brain-cathedral rose window white in D. Normal renders weren't affected.

It has a heavy outer rule, a guilloche band all round (gold top and bottom, green down the sides) between fine double rules, microprint along its inner edge, a fine rule round the picture, and large rosettes in the corners.
- **The subtitles:** the banderole now sits in the note's bottom band, like the panel where a real note's value goes.

1. **Recommended, a gag on "No office buzzing in my palm":** her phone lies face down on the coffee table and starts buzzing, rattling with doodled buzz lines. 3. **The page drawing itself:** her pencil adds small things to the doodle on the beat (slippers on her feet, a plant, the sun's smile), because it's her imagined day.

> 1. if you're showing the screen, you need to cut the camera to her perspective looking down at the phone. We won't be able to see the screen in this doodle style, and the phone shouldn't face the camera (and, as a result, away from her. This should be an animation rule btw)

**Claude** <sub>11:21</sub>

Understood. That's a rule worth writing down. I'll tell the agent now and add it to the polish checklist:

Got it, and it's now a written rule. To show what's on a screen, we cut to the user's point of view, looking down past their hand. A screen is never turned to the camera to show its content, since that turns it away from its user, and its content never floats out of it in a wide shot. The only exception is your V2a case: a phone held low at someone's side, not in use, can show its face in passing. It's in `brief/polish_checklist.md` next to "screens face whoever is using them", committed and pushed.

| `H_skip3_bar` | the same, with a slow full-bar crossfade |
| `I_skip3_fade` | the same, but the bridge fades out and the coda comes in on its note |
| `C_as_is_halfbar` | your pick, C, for comparison |

In all four new versions the ending comes in on the fourth note, the phrase you want to keep. The three notes that jarred (a high F, a low F and a G) are gone. The bridge runs on for about 2.5 seconds to fill their place, so the credits stay the same length and end exactly where they did.

My guess is that F or H will sound most natural.

**Claude** <sub>20:15</sub>

Two parts: a 16:9 cover for YouTube, and upload encode settings. I'll look at how the cover page handles its formats, and at what the encoder does now:

The 16:9 cover goes to an agent: it's layout work in the cover page, which nothing else is touching. I'll do the encode settings myself, starting with how the frames are written:

The flags are in. Checking the exact ffmpeg arguments on 2 seconds of real 4K frames with the song, then reading back what the file reports:

I've set up a 4K YouTube upload preset, and an agent is building the 16:9 cover.

**YouTube upload encode (committed):** a new `--youtube` preset in the renderer, following YouTube's recommended upload settings for 4K SDR:

| | Setting |
|---|---|
| Video | H.264 High profile, level 5.1, 3840×2160, 24 fps progressive |
| GOP | closed, a keyframe every 12 frames (half the frame rate), 2 B-frames |
| Quality | CRF 14, about 70 Mbps. YouTube recommends 35–45 Mbps for 4K24; a higher-quality upload survives its re-encode better. |
| Colour | BT.709, limited range, tagged explicitly |
| Audio | AAC-LC, 48 kHz stereo, 384 kbps |
| Container | MP4, with the index at the front (`faststart`) |

- **Colour fix:** the rendered frames are JPEGs in a different colour standard (BT.601, full range). Our normal encodes pass that through, and YouTube handles it unreliably, which can shift colours slightly. The preset converts it properly to BT.709. I tested it on 2 seconds of real 4K frames: every tag reads correctly, and the decoded picture is within about 1.5 levels of the frames.
- **Sharper frames:** the final 4K frames will be saved at higher JPEG quality (.97 instead of .94), so there's less compression going into the upload.
- **4K matters even for 1080p viewers:** a 4K upload gets YouTube's higher-quality VP9/AV1 streams at every resolution, so it helps 1080p viewers too.

**16:9 cover:** an agent is laying out the "worlds" cover at 3840×2160, with and without the title. It'll also make a 1280×720 thumbnail under 2 MB, which is what YouTube accepts. It's designed to read at thumbnail size: a big title, readable faces, and nothing important in the bottom-right corner, where YouTube puts the duration badge.

**The final render**, once the carriage crowd fix lands, is one script:
1. It refreshes the Claude usage tally.
2. It renders the 1080p master.
3. It renders the 4K master.
4. It encodes `All That Time Saved - UncleHerbert - 4K YouTube upload.mp4` from the 4K frames.

The masters rendering now (from before the crowd fix) will finish in about 45 minutes. They're still useful to watch, but they'll be superseded.

I've tightened the trademark note in the credits, and it's committed and pushed. It now reads, in her small print:

> Claude, Copilot, Gemini, Grok, Muse and Codex, and their mascots, are trademarks of Anthropic, Microsoft, Google, xAI, Meta and OpenAI respectively.
> This film is an independent work of parody and commentary. It is not affiliated with, sponsored or endorsed by any of them.

The existing "In caricature" section still says none of the real people were involved.

**How the rights work.** This is general information, not legal advice; for a commercial release, a short check with an IP lawyer is worth it. I've framed it for UK law, where I assume you are.

- **Names (trademarks):** saying "Grok" or "Copilot" to refer to those products, in commentary, is fine. The real risk is implying they endorse or are involved with the film, and the new "not affiliated, sponsored or endorsed" line addresses that directly. Don't use their actual logos as your branding.
- **Mascot designs (copyright):** the companies own their mascot artwork. Ours are new drawings made "after" them, redrawn in five styles and used to comment on the companies. UK law has specific exceptions for caricature, parody and pastiche, and for criticism; US fair use favours parody. It's weaker ground if the bots get used outside the film, for example on merchandise or as a channel logo.
- **Real people (Musk, Zuckerberg, Altman, Dorsey and the others):** the UK has no general right to control your own likeness, and satirising public figures is well established. The risks are defamation, meaning a false statement presented as fact, and implied endorsement. The lyrics are opinion, and the turbine permits reflect what was reported; the credits say none of them was involved.

**Releasing the source under MIT.** Some things to sort out first:
1. **LICENSE:** the current file is the framework's, "Copyright (c) 2026 John Heibel". Keep his notice for his code, and add your own copyright line for the project's code.
2. **MIT only covers what you own, which is the code.** Say so in the README, and keep the music, lyrics, artwork and the film itself "all rights reserved", or under something like CC BY-NC-ND if you want sharing but not reuse.
   - **The song, instrumental and yodel cover:** ownership of Suno output depends on your plan when it was generated. Paid plans assign it to you.
   - **The lyrics:** check Astra's terms.
3. **Third-party reference images:** `brief/ref/` contains the companies' own mascot art: `meta_muse_jolly_ref.png`, `meta_muse_charm_*_ref.png` and the Codex pets sheets. Those shouldn't be published. Because they're in the git history, deleting them now isn't enough. We'd either publish a fresh copy of the repo without that history, or rewrite the history to remove them. I'd do the fresh copy when you're ready to release.
4. **Fonts:** the fonts are under the SIL Open Font License, and their licence files are already in `assets/fonts/`, so they're fine to include.

I can draft the README section and the LICENSE addition, and prepare the clean public copy, whenever you want.
